# Decisions

The source of truth for what is settled. Other docs must agree with this one.

**Who decides:** design decisions (gameplay, art, scope, what the game is) belong to the developers. Technical decisions (engine, architecture, tooling, workflow) are made by the agent, recorded here with the reason, and can be overruled. The Owner column says which kind a decision is.

**Who made it:** the By column says who made the call. **Developers**: they said it, chose it or agreed to it; only they change it. **Agent**: an AI agent chose it, a technical question or a design question the developers left to it; it is a soft decision. An agent may change a soft decision when it has a reason, recording the reason and a log line, without asking; anything the developers say overrules it; and when they confirm one, it becomes theirs. If the developers asked for something and the agent chose how, the how is the agent's. Open, superseded and rejected items have no one (—).

**Everything here is provisional.** This is pre-production: the whole record is our current thinking, written down so we can keep working from it, and any of it may change. The statuses say how firm an item is relative to the others, not that anything is locked.

**Decided**: our current answer; don't reopen without a reason. **Leaning**: working assumption, untested or unconfirmed. **Open**: not chosen. **Rejected**: considered and dropped. **Superseded**: replaced by a later decision. Rejected and superseded items are kept in their own section below the main table, so their numbers still resolve.

A new decision goes in its area's section with the next free number. **By area:** [The game as a whole](#the-game-as-a-whole) · [Worlds and realms](#worlds-and-realms) · [The player and survival](#the-player-and-survival) · [Items and crafting](#items-and-crafting) · [Lore, magic, combat and growth](#lore-magic-combat-and-growth) · [People and the tribe](#people-and-the-tribe) · [Building](#building) · [Art, characters and sound](#art-characters-and-sound) · [Engine and code](#engine-and-code) · [Tools and workflow](#tools-and-workflow) · [Superseded and rejected](#superseded-and-rejected) · [Log](#log)

## The game as a whole

What we are building, its tenets, scope and players.

| # | Topic | Owner | By | Status | Decision | Why, or what closes it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D1 | Team and assets | Design | Developers | Decided | Two developers. Every asset is free, bought, or LLM-generated. Code is LLM-written. | Sets scope. Favours procedural content and agent-friendly tooling. |
| D2 | Multiplayer | Design | Developers | Decided | Multiplayer, with no PvP. Friends playing together is the small case of a persistent server holding up to a thousand (D52). | Too expensive to retrofit, so it is in the architecture from the first build. Built server-first (D51). Was "co-op, up to 4 players" until 2026-10-10. |
| D3 | Tone | Design | Developers | Decided | Challenging, with goals to prepare for rather than punishment: far gentler than Vintage Story, and never bleak. Threats are answered by fighting, building and holding ground with recruits (D90). | Planet Crafter, the first half's old yardstick, belonged to the terraforming pitch and was dropped on 2026-10-12 ("planet crafter is dead yeah"). The "challenging, with goals" wording is the developers' own, of the starter world (D62). The second half, "Factorio-style: threats are answered with automation", was replaced by D90 on 2026-10-11; the developers did not recognise it as theirs. |
| D7 | Platforms | Design | Developers | Decided | Cross-platform, and above all cross-platform to develop: Windows (via WSL) and macOS. | |
| D8 | Procedural content | Design | Developers | Decided | Procedural generation is the default for terrain, flora, sky and anything else it can cover. | Follows from D1. |
| D10 | Browser | Design | Developers | Decided | Nice to have, not a requirement. It does not constrain the engine choice. | |
| D15 | Astroneer | Design | Developers | Decided | Not a model for gameplay, and not the art target. It is only evidence that this tech is achievable by a small team. | Its gameplay isn't fun; its art is fine but not the bar. |
| D18 | Fail state | Design | Developers | Decided | None. Setbacks only. | Confirmed by the developers on 2026-10-11: it is a persistent sandbox. Was Leaning, following from D3. |
| D38 | Licence | Design | Developers | Decided | MIT, for everything in this repository. | Decided by the developers on 2026-10-10. The repository is public, and without a licence nobody else could legally use it. It also matches Voxel Tools, which we port from. |
| D49 | How we work from here | Design | Developers | Leaning | Larger design docs come before implementation. Experiments stay small and are not a substitute for a design. | The developers said on 2026-10-10 that it would probably be better to work on larger design docs than to implement things one after another. AGENTS.md treats it as a rule. |
| D51 | Server-first multiplayer | Design | Developers | Decided | The game always talks to a server, which decides everything and sends each player what is near them. For friends playing together the server runs inside one player's game; for a persistent world it runs alone. Both are the same code. Make a handful of players work first, then measure larger worlds before promising them. | Agreed by the developers on 2026-10-10 as the safest way. It keeps a large persistent world possible without building all of it now. See [multiplayer.md](multiplayer.md). |
| D52 | How many players a world holds | Design | Developers | Decided | A thousand concurrent players on one persistent server is what everything is built toward. It is a test each decision has to pass, not a promise for a date. The world they share is fully procedural and has to be very cheap to compute, send and store. | Set by the developers on 2026-10-10, raised from "ideally 100" earlier the same day. It replaces the limit of four in D2. Nothing has been measured at any scale; what it asks of the architecture and the design is in [multiplayer.md](multiplayer.md). |
| D85 | Our inspirations | Design | Developers | Decided | Kenshi, Enshrouded, Subnautica 2, *He Who Fights With Monsters*, Soulmask, Red Dead Redemption 2 for a world that feels alive, The Forest for its building, Terraria for discovering and unlocking, Dune: Awakening for its graphics, atmosphere and lore, and Sons of the Forest for its world design. What we take from each is in [vision.md](vision.md#our-inspirations). | Named by the developers on 2026-10-11; Red Dead Redemption 2 added by them on 2026-10-12, "as the world feels very alive"; The Forest the same day, for "a nice building system, the way you get logs/etc is nice for larger projects"; and Terraria, "similar to how we'll have realms… you go to the other zones, find new materials, unlock new kinds of things you can craft and build (lots would just be visual aesthetic)"; and Dune: Awakening, "great graphics, 'ok' building system, cool atmosphere and good lore"; and Sons of the Forest, whose world design is "beautiful, full, looks organic". Was "Enshrouded and Subnautica are probably the two biggest" (2026-10-10). A game's look is separate from its design and systems: taking Enshrouded's systems does not mean taking its look, which is the art direction's question (D14). Building in voxels is settled (D9). The tension with D3 was settled by D90. |
| D89 | Direction: a living world | Design | Developers | Decided | The game's design is [gameplay.md](gameplay.md) and the system pages it links (once game design 001): still a sandbox with some survival, but focused on a living world you take part in, with NPCs, monsters, wildlife and events, across realms reached by portals; closer to Enshrouded, a sandbox with RPG in it; *He Who Fights With Monsters* as the basis of the lore, as world-building rather than content ([lore.md](lore.md), D125). No terraforming, and no oxygen tanks or science fiction for now. After this file, those pages are the source of truth for design; each item in them is as settled as the decision it cites. | Considered by the developers on 2026-10-11; adopted on 2026-10-12: "we're doing realms per the game design doc, which you can consider now the source of truth and can be interleaved elsewhere". Its pages moved from proposals into docs, and the pitch (vision.md), survival, progression and the old gameplay page were brought in line. |
| D92 | No hand-holding | Design | Developers | Decided | A principle of the game: it does not hold your hand. There may be quests, but not a tracked quest log that points you where to go, and no journal: what to remember is up to the player. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11 as "one principle of the game". Proposed: quests are what people tell you, directions come from landmarks, and the map shows what you have seen and marked; see [gameplay.md](gameplay.md#no-hand-holding). Bears on the map (D76). |
| D93 | Rich systems, little on screen | Design | Developers | Decided | A principle: the game is rich with systems but somewhat realistic. You do not have a magic display of every piece of information, as you would not in life; you learn things by looking, doing and being told. Some compromises are made so it stays fun. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11. The developers added the same day that there will still be some on-screen display and character stats; D93 bounds how much, it does not remove it. See [gameplay.md](gameplay.md#no-hand-holding). |
| D105 | The MVP | Design | Agent | Leaning | A first playable, as a prototype of the core loop in [gameplay.md](gameplay.md): one world and biome, gathering with tools, crafting, a claim and voxel building, one wildlife and one monster, combat, one settlement and a rescue, skills, hunger and death, a local server, saving. No essences in it; they come first after it. | Proposed 2026-10-11 after the developers left essences in the MVP to me. Scope and pass criteria in [prototypes.md](prototypes.md#mvp-the-first-playable), for the developers to edit. |
| D107 | Disclosing AI | Design | Developers | Decided | The game says plainly that its assets are made with AI, on Steam's disclosure and wherever else it applies. How players or the press react is not a reason to avoid a tool. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11: "We'd disclose that everything is AI so that's not a big deal, I don't care about that." Follows from D1. Steam requires disclosing content made with AI that ships in the game; see [research/ai-models-for-art.md](../research/ai-models-for-art.md#rights-and-reception). |
| D122 | Realistic but fun | Design | Developers | Decided | A core tenet of the game: realistic but fun. Things work as they would in the world, so players can reason about them; where realism would only be a chore, fun wins. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11, to stand in AGENTS.md. Gathers what D93 (somewhat realistic, little on screen) and D3 (no chores) already said. |
| D125 | Our own design, from many sources | Design | Developers | Decided | The core mechanics of *He Who Fights With Monsters* and its approach to world-building can be used as they are (D87, D98). The specifics, such as which styles, disciplines, essences and peoples there are, we invent ourselves, drawing heavily on literature and game design for inspiration. The lore is written out in our own words in [lore.md](lore.md): the books' fundamental systems of how the universe works, and their names for those systems (ranks, essences, confluences, awakening stones, spirit coins, quintessence, auras, the astral), but never their content: the actual lists of essences, abilities, stones, confluences, monsters, peoples, places and gods are ours. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11, of combat styles and the books; restated on 2026-10-12: "the core foundational lore will be from He Who Fights With Monsters, but we won't literally do item for item from those books. We'll just use its world building for the fundamental systems that exist in the universe", to be very clear in the design docs and written out in our own words. Clarified the same day: "what I mean by don't copy the book is the actual essences etc.; we will come up with our own lists". |
| D127 | Many of everything | Design | Developers | Decided | A core principle: assume a large quantity of every kind of object in the game, because it is procedurally generated. Every system is built to perform, and stay modular and flexible, with many of each kind of thing and many kinds: items, gear, creatures, characters, props, places. Adding one more kind is data, not code; nothing is written for one particular object. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12, as a core part of a procedural game, while gear was being fitted to the characters. Builds on D39 (procedural generation almost everywhere) and D58 (the game modelled in ECS). |
| D182 | Hundreds of variants, not millions | Design | Developers | Decided | A core tenet: almost everything is modular and procedural, but thematic. Things are made from parts by rules, as Borderlands makes guns and No Man's Sky makes planets, and every variant belongs to a theme (a realm, a maker, a people) that shapes which parts and rules it draws from. Aim for hundreds or thousands of variants of a kind, each different in a way a player notices and cares about, not millions of near-copies made only to be numerous. A generator says how many distinct variants it makes and what tells them apart; when it makes more than players can tell apart, it is narrowed or given more structure rather than widened. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "almost everything in this game we want to be modular and procedural, but to be thematic ... we dont want infinite low value things just to have infinite things. so think 100s or 1000s of variants, not millions", as a core tenet to keep work on track. D127 says systems must cope with many of everything; this says how many is worth making. Answers what vision.md already left of No Man's Sky (its infinite universe and shallow planets) and gives D59 (every world thematic) a rule for everything else. |
| D187 | What we are building | Design | Developers | Decided | First and foremost, an extremely ambitious sandbox engine, with multitudes of procedural worlds and intricate systems for each component of the game. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12, in those words. |

## Worlds and realms

Ground, water, weather, seasons, life and how realms join.

| # | Topic | Owner | By | Status | Decision | Why, or what closes it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D4 | World paradigm | Design | Developers | Decided | Many realms (D17, D87), each a round world, seamless to walk with no loading screens within a world. No space: no solar system and no flight to orbit. | Changed by the developers on 2026-10-10: "no space". Was: real round planets in a real solar system, seamless from surface to space, after Space Engineers and No Man's Sky. Confirmed the same day: each world is still a round planet. |
| D13 | World scale | Design | Agent | Leaning | Always round, fully procedural, and large but finite: walking all the way round should be a ridiculous task. Proposed: about 30 km radius for the starter world (189 km round, 12 hours on foot), and 10 to 50 km for others, set by theme. | The developers set the intent on 2026-10-10 and asked for numbers that feel good; these are a proposal until walked. Rendering cost measured the same at 2 km and 20 km in the retired Godot spike. Needs positions relative to a moving origin, and 64-bit on the server (D36). See [worlds.md](worlds.md#size). Was "about 2 km", which is under an hour on foot. |
| D17 | Worlds | Design | Developers | Decided | Many worlds, each different from the others, with no fixed limit: realms, as in *He Who Fights With Monsters*, reached probably by portals (D72). Not a promise to ship unlimited, but nothing in the design constrains the number. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11: "the dozen limit is gone since we're not doing the solar system". Was: several planets, probably about a dozen, not thousands (2026-10-10). Every world must therefore come from rules and its seed alone, and cost nothing until someone is in it; see [worlds.md](worlds.md#what-it-asks-of-the-engine). |
| D20 | Only players reshape the ground | Design | Developers | Decided | The solid ground changes only when players dig. | Decided by the developers, and confirmed on 2026-10-10. It also avoids replaying every edit when terrain changes. |
| D43 | What can be harvested | Design | Agent | Leaning | Possibly rock and ore by mining, trees by chopping, grass by cutting. Likely every plant can be harvested except short ground cover; tall grass and reeds can, giving fibre (2026-10-12). Things grow back (D150). | The engine supports all three kinds (ground, objects, cover) through one contract, so this can stay open. See [engine.md](engine.md). |
| D59 | World variety | Design | Developers | Decided | Every world is thematic and varied: its own terrain, caverns, water features and foliage. A world is a seed and a theme; a new world is a new theme, not new code. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10. The "theme as data" part is my proposal in [worlds.md](worlds.md). |
| D62 | The starter world | Design | Developers | Decided | Breathable air and the like: the easiest world, where a player begins. Challenging, not a freebie: its winters pose real problems, with goals to prepare for, gentler than Vintage Story's (D3). | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10 as what it is "for now"; on 2026-10-12, plainly: "starter world should be challenging, not a freebie, so winter would pose challenges already. Vintage Story makes it a little brutal, but like we want you to have goals". See [climate.md](climate.md#winter-on-the-starter-world). |
| D63 | Gravity | Design | Developers | Decided | Worlds have gravity, toward their centre. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10. Whether its strength differs by world is open. |
| D72 | Getting between worlds | Design | Developers | Leaning | Settled on 2026-10-12: there is no fast travel between claims. Quick travel comes from other systems, such as magic (astral magic), portals players build and mounts, and what you carry goes with you. Leaning on the rest: probably portals, or a similar mechanism. The first is likely a portal you find in the world; more ways of crossing come over time, perhaps portals players build, as in Valheim. Not flight: there is no space (D4). No traditional loading screen when crossing; the crossing itself, a portal effect, may cover loading and unloading if it has to. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10. Was flight through space, seamless and without a loading screen, Decided earlier the same day. Whether crossing has a loading screen, and how the server hands a player between worlds, are open; see [worlds.md](worlds.md#realms-and-portals). |
| D75 | Sea level | Design | Developers | Decided | Every world with water has a sea level, and the sea behaves as physics says it should: its surface is level, the same height everywhere on the world under that world's gravity, and water fills the ground below it wherever the sea can reach. How deep it is depends only on the ground beneath (D74). | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10. My reading of "as physics says": a hollow below sea level that the sea cannot reach stays dry, and buoyancy and pressure follow from depth. Flowing water is not planned. Replaces D24. See [worlds.md](worlds.md#what-the-developers-want). |
| D78 | Weather | Design | Developers | Decided | There are weather systems, and weather differs by world. What weather there is, and what it does to the player, are open. Players can forecast it, mostly by looking; technology and magic can add to it (2026-10-12). | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10. See [worlds.md](worlds.md#weather); proposed in [climate.md](climate.md): weather as a function of the world's seed and time, so the server sends only the time. |
| D86 | Across realms | Design | Developers | Decided | One art style across the whole game (D14); cultures differ in their models and details, made by generation and theme. Your gear is your gear in every realm. Settings and cultures per realm, and recruiting from them, are D116 and D161. | Said plainly by the developers on 2026-10-10, among ideas marked "might", "maybe" and "idk"; those ideas (more than one breathable realm, settings such as fantasy or futuristic, cultures that unlock things) are carried by D116, D161 and [settings.md](settings.md). |
| D87 | Realms | Design | Developers | Decided | Realms, not a set number of planets, in the spirit of *He Who Fights With Monsters*: no space between them (D4), reached probably by portals (D72), and no terraforming (D89). | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10, first as "maybe", then as "still different realms or planets or something similar". Unaffected: procedural worlds with their own themes and cultures (D59, D86), voxel ground, survival, building, items, skills. Each realm is still a round world (D4). Confirmed on 2026-10-12: "we are not doing 12 planets, we're doing realms per the game design doc". See [worlds.md](worlds.md#realms-and-portals). |
| D91 | Reasons to explore and settle | Design | Developers | Decided | The player must have reasons to explore, and to need to settle in more than one place. The mechanism is open; how travel works is D72. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11. Proposed levers (resources by region, held ground, outposts as the travel network, a small backpack, people and magic sites spread through the wild, the spread) are in [gameplay.md](gameplay.md#why-explore-and-why-settle-in-more-than-one-place). |
| D110 | A life-sized world | Design | Developers | Decided | The world feels life-sized: things are at real-world scale, so trees stand as tall as real trees, rocks range up to boulders bigger than a person, and hills and mountains are as large as real ones. The player is small against the land. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11 as a design requirement, after scene tests where trees and hills read as miniatures. It applies to every model's size (the pipeline sets each one's height in metres) and to terrain generation, and it fits the world's scale (D13). See [art-style.md](art-style.md#life-sized). |
| D116 | Settings and generated realms | Design | Developers | Leaning | Every realm's theme fills the same gameplay roles, as models fill palette roles, so dozens of generated realms fit one lore; rank is the only power axis. Realms differ in how much magic they have. A theme's weapons differ in how they play, as swords from staves, not in strength. Loot, procedural loot included, may become part of the game, not a large part. Settings may mix within a realm later, carefully. How realms are explained is left to the generator. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11, reacting to [settings.md](settings.md): "roughly on point". Each setting's speciality is undecided. |
| D134 | Day and night | Design | — | Open | Recommended: a whole day lasts half an hour of play, a little more day than night, as at a middle latitude in early summer; night dark enough that light matters but never so dark the ground is lost. Open: what night does besides being dark, such as more or stranger monsters, and cold ([survival.md](survival.md), [creatures.md](creatures.md)). | Built in the tech demo on 2026-10-12 with these numbers, which are constants to change. The developers asked for a torch in the MVP. |
| D148 | How a world fills with life | Design | Developers | Decided | The goals in [biomes.md](biomes.md), with the developers' answers of 2026-10-12: forests as dense as Sons of the Forest's; biomes made by rules, never chosen from a list (proposed: species with niches competing for ground); worn paths a stretch goal; leaves as cards or clumps undecided. "Realistic" is the forest's density, not the look: the developers like Sons of the Forest's art style but think it likely beyond us, so D14 stands. The technical approach (objects, cover and far tiers; rules as a stack of fields; placement after Horizon Zero Dawn) is mine and Leaning until the first step measures it. Leaves as cards or clumps are chosen by eye later. | Asked for by the developers on 2026-10-12 after naming Sons of the Forest for its world design (D85): "Sons of the Forest is more realistic, and that's our goal"; "(worn paths) would be fucking amazing as a goal, but probably hard?"; biomes "TBD, remember procedural is the goal". Agreed the same day: "if we've locked in those decisions that's already great". Today plants are set once around the start and the ground beyond is bare. See [research/procedural-vegetation.md](../research/procedural-vegetation.md). |
| D301 | The lush forest a tall conifer stand, looming | Design | Developers | Decided | The lush forest is a tall, open conifer stand as Sons of the Forest's: Scots pines and spruces rising high on long clear trunks, their crowns far overhead, so a player walks among trunks and looks up into them; oaks stand in it as the exception, not as a closed low canopy. The floor under it gets more light, and grass with it (D246, D293). | The developers on 2026-10-14, asked whether the forest should be a SotF-like tall conifer stand with oaks as the exception or stay an oak wood: "i think it should be like it, as in its pretty large looming thing, i do enjoy that pretty much." Research: [2026-10-14-sotf-forest-look.md](../research/2026-10-14-sotf-forest-look.md), recommendation 5. |
| D149 | Seasons | Design | Developers | Decided | Worlds have seasons, and they change play, not only the look. One season covers a whole world: no opposite hemispheres. Seasons are fundamentals, not hardcoded concepts: a slow swing in temperature, daylight and precipitation, with weather on top, and bodies, ground, plants and creatures respond to those quantities, so cold is cold and hot is hot on any world, and winter is not snow all the time. A year's length is per world and changeable; it starts at 365 days, about 7.6 real days. See [climate.md](climate.md). | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "yes we will have seasons"; "hemispheres having opposite seasons seems complex and not totally useful"; "seasons SHOULD affect play"; "cold is cold, hot is hot, but remember, procedural multiple realms so it's about fundamentals not about hardcoded simplistic concepts"; the year "we should be able to change/configure, and you can pick something that fits the rest of the design", and "winter isn't snow 100% of the time". Sons of the Forest has seasons (D85). |
| D150 | Regrowth | Design | Developers | Decided | What is cut or harvested grows back, each kind at its own pace, set as data. By default nothing grows back inside a claim, and removing a stump stops what was cut from regrowing; both to revisit if they cause trouble. How is open: at minimum by time passed, and perhaps seen to grow rather than coming back whole. Proposed in [biomes.md](biomes.md#regrowth): in stages worked out from the time since, changing only out of sight, the stored change forgotten once grown. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "yes things should grow back but we have to figure out that system yet… at minimum time passed, but you might need to 'see' them grow back vs be instantly full regrown"; "the speed of growth will need to vary based on what it is"; "maybe by default nothing regrows in claim and we can redecide that later"; "removing a stump can prevent it from regrowing and that's fine". Answers part of D43. |
| D174 | Felling trees | Design | Developers | Decided | Trees behave as in The Forest: chopped down, and all of it seen. Proposed in [biomes.md](biomes.md#felling-a-tree): a notch deepens with each blow, the tree tips away from the cut and falls through small plants, its trunk lies in logs where it fell (D140), branches and leaves scatter, and a stump stays to regrow (D150). Felling is one operation the server decides; the fall is shown on each machine and lands the same everywhere; generated trees are made in parts for it. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "trees we're going to need to behave like The Forest btw where we can chop them down etc and that's all visual". The Forest is already an inspiration for how logs are got (D85). |
| D321 | Biome demos: a mountain lake, a tundra, a living town | Design | Agent | Leaning | The next demos after the lush forest, as [demos.md](demos.md) says, each made by the world's rules on the 30 km world in the start's climate, the sandbox choosing only the place, the date, the hour and the camera. **A mountain lake:** a lake in a valley whose forest gives out with height where the growing season is too cold for its trees, the tree line emerging from species' niches (D313), each tree shorter and sparser toward its limit and then krummholz, above it alpine meadow, scree, rock and snow by the date. **A tundra:** where the world's climate puts it, about 11 km north of the start, its ground cover five communities by exposure and wetness, the same cover as the alpine meadow. **A living town:** a market town of 30 to 40 buildings round a square, about ten roles of people keeping their days, and domestic animals. The open choices (heights, seasons, trees, the town, animals, voices) are demos.md's questions. | The developers on 2026-10-15: "I would like to see a forest with a lake in the mountains, i want mountains to have both foresty part and the high peaks. i would like to see more of a tundra effect, as a final effect i would like to see a town that is bustling with life - has NPCs, maybe some animals, etc." The specifics are the agent's recommendation, Leaning until they answer. Research: [2026-10-15-biome-demos.md](../research/2026-10-15-biome-demos.md). |
| D350 | The market town paved, with shops, women and a horizon | Design | Developers | Decided | Amends D321's town. The town's streets and square are paved (setts of the local stone laid in packed earth, earth and mud where the paving gives out); stalls and shops round the square sell varied goods, food among them, drawn per stall by its seed; its people vary, women among them; and from its streets and square the distance is more town or forest and mountains, never empty. Its people walk. | The developers, 2026-10-15, after watching round 2: "re town: brick road, town square, shops, a few different NPCs, women etc. shops, with random merchandise, food etc. and its not walk, it's still run. in the distance it should either still be a town, or have some sort of fauna on the horizon / mountains in the horizon. We want to make this visually appealing." |

## The player and survival

Needs, death, the body, the map and options.

| # | Topic | Owner | By | Status | Decision | Why, or what closes it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D61 | Survival attributes | Design | Developers | Decided | Health, stamina, breath (D74), hunger and thirst, coupled to survival, and able to work differently on each world. More may be added: mana is D102, and body temperature D153. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10. How each works is a proposal in [survival.md](survival.md). |
| D74 | Swimming and breathing | Design | Developers | Decided | The character can swim, using stamina for now. Under water, breath is used up as it is on a world with thin or no air: one rule for anywhere the air cannot be breathed. No set limit on depth: water should be flexible. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10. Needs an animated swim, and water you can be in, which the sea (D75) already gives. See [survival.md](survival.md). |
| D76 | A map | Design | Developers | Decided | There is a map system. It shows only where you have been: the rest is hidden until explored. For now it shows where you are; whether to hide that, as Outward does, is for later. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10. Questions in [player.md](player.md#the-map). |
| D80 | Options menu | Design | Developers | Decided | An options menu with the settings players expect, including audio and remapping keys. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10. Proposed contents in [player.md](player.md#options). A first version is built in the game (`terra_game`): every control is looked up by action, and settings are a plain `name = value` file in the player's settings folder, read with no new dependency and ignored by scripted runs so they stay repeatable. |
| D101 | Food and death | Design | Developers | Decided | Food is essential: going without weakens you and in the end kills you. Once hunger or thirst is empty, health wears away slowly, over days, and your attributes are weaker meanwhile; starving takes three in-game days, and thirst two. The same for recruits (D169). Death costs a little progress toward your next step of skills, never a step, rank, essence or ability (D171), besides what you carried (D84). Still no fail state (D18). Whether good food gives buffs is for later. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11; on 2026-10-12, of recruits and players alike: "their hunger gauge is fine, but like it would slowly eat away at health over days, not crazy fast. same w/ the player. but it'd slow their attributes or make them weaker"; then "3 in game days seems fine", and of thirst, "i guess 2 days is fine". The death cost was settled with D171. See [survival.md](survival.md). |
| D111 | Character creator | Design | Developers | Decided | A creator of a few sliders, not templates: sex, build (heavier, thinner, stronger), height, and skin colour; hairstyles and hair colour. Height varies widely, enough for other races and humanoid types later; other proportions (limb length, shoulders, head size) not yet. Hairstyle is not tied to the body: it can be changed in the game later, as Enshrouded's barber does. Equipment is visible when worn. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11: "we don't need a ton of sliders.. diff hair, skin colors, slightly diff body templates", after Enshrouded. On 2026-10-12 they settled sliders only, rather than templates or both; height "varied ... otherwise we won't be able to have varied races/humanoid types"; proportions beyond height not yet. Each slider is a weight of the base body's morphs, a uniform scale or a skin tint (D112), so every item fits every setting; the named templates stay as presets for people and tests. |
| D153 | Body temperature | Design | Developers | Decided | A survival attribute of its own, for players and recruits alike (D169). It drifts toward what the surroundings push it to (air, wind, wet, sun, shelter, fire), slowed by clothing and raised by work. Out of a comfortable band it costs you: cold or heat raises stamina costs, and slows a recruit's work (D169). Proposed: cold also raises hunger and heat thirst, and far out of the band health falls; shown on the body first (D93). | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "body temperature is probably useful, and basically if you're too cold it could increase stamina usage (same with too hot) or increase thirst or something else"; settled with D169 the same day: recruits "need to deal w/ temperature as well as itll slow down their work", and "npcs generally should be pretty similar to a player". See [climate.md](climate.md#body-temperature). |
| D312 | Climate shared where it changes play, each machine's where it is only seen | Design | Developers | Decided | What a season or the weather does to play is worked out the same on every machine by the shared crate (D28): the temperature where a body stands, snow depth, ice that holds a walker, wet ground, what a plant offers to gather. What it does only to the look (how far a leaf has turned, frost, breath in the air) is each machine's own. See [climate.md](climate.md#shared-and-seen-d312). | The developers on 2026-10-14, asked whether to share everything that changes play and keep the rest visual: "yeah your recommendation re 1 is correct." |
| D313 | What grows where and when: species' niches and years, the world's climate | Design | Agent | Decided | A species carries, as data, its niche (the climate it tolerates: coldest winter, warmth to grow, wetness, shade, soil) and its year (when it leafs out, flowers, fruits, turns and drops its leaves, by warmth and chill counted and the day's length). The world decides what grows where by setting its climate fields against those niches; a biome is what emerges, named afterwards, and a world's theme says which families of species exist there at all. A place whose climate a kind does not tolerate does not regrow it (D150). Vintage Story places and colours its plants by climate so; Far Cry 5 lets species compete for ground (biomes.md). See [climate.md](climate.md#what-grows-where-and-when-d313). | Left to the agent by the developers on 2026-10-14: "if we have to swap plants within the world, idk how that works. world has to somehow control what grows when were/maybe thats a function fo biome, idk actually, do whats best practice." |
| D314 | Climate in code: fields, an integrated past, samples and maps | Tech | Agent | Decided | Temperature, daylight, precipitation, wind and the air's wetness are pure functions of a place and a time in a Bevy-free crate, from the world's seed and clock, so the server sends only the time; snow, wetness, ice and plants' warmth and chill are folded over recent weather per coarse cell, kept by the day, never saved; things that care carry a `ClimateSample` refreshed by distance from players, bent by local components (fire, shelter, shade, clothing) and read by small systems (a body's temperature, creatures, walking, ice); plants do not tick but are worked out from their cell, their kind's year and their seed; the renderer reads climate maps baked round the camera, as it reads the cover map. Built in the order in climate.md, proved in the lush forest. | The developers on 2026-10-14: "i like treating it systemic as in there's a temperature measure and that affects the world, like think of how this would work in ECS"; "/goal around all of that, im fine with it." Costs a few texture reads on the GPU and a fold per cell per day on the CPU. |
| D315 | Climate's crate, clock, weather and cells | Tech | Agent | Decided | `terra_climate`, free of Bevy (D28). The world's clock is days (`WorldTime`, a 64-bit count, the day its whole part), all the server sends of the climate (D52); a world's climate is a data file (`assets/data/climate.ron` for the start). The year's slow terms (the sun's path, the season's warmth and wetness) move on once a day, at midnight. Weather is two layers of `terra_gen` noise drifting at different speeds, fronts a theme's size across passing in a theme's spell of days; something falls where they pass a threshold set by the share of the time it should, snow below 0 °C, rain above 2, mixed between. The past is folded per 32 m cell at eight samples a day, from the first midsummer at least half a year back (0.5 to 1.5 years): warmth (degree-days above 5 °C since midwinter) and chill (below 10 °C since midsummer) are exact from their resets, snow and ice are none at midsummer, and the ground's water forgets its start within the fold; each cell is kept as of the last day asked. The clock starts in late August, the sun 10° north as the art was lit, and the sun's path follows the date. | Standard models, so the numbers mean what they do in the field: snow melts by Hock's enhanced temperature index (1999), ice grows by Stefan's law, the ground dries by Hamon's evapotranspiration, the mean falls toward the poles as in Held and Suarez's idealised Earth. Late August rather than mid-April, the other date of that sun: the summer's leaves and warmth the forest was tuned under, not spring's bare oaks. Measured: 1.8 µs a cell a day, 0.65 ms a cell folded afresh. |
| D327 | Climate samples, maps and plants' years in code | Tech | Agent | Decided | `terra_world::climate` (D314's shape). A `ClimateSample` on what cares (the walker's player now) is taken again every half second within 48 m of a player, every 3 s within 200 m and every 20 s beyond, at most 0.6 ms of a frame; local things bend it as components the layers above give: `Heat` (a fire's radiant watts, spread over a sphere round it), `Sheltered` (0 to 1: the wind to a tenth, nothing falling and no sun, a fire's heat held in, doubled) and the canopy, from the sky occlusion volume (D270: the sun cut by the sky hidden, the rain and wind to two fifths where it is closed). The past at a point is its 32 m cell's, taken at the cell's middle so every machine asks the same (`ClimateFields::place`, where D323's aspect, drifting and latitude from where it is will come in; the start's latitude everywhere until then), going from its day's start to the next day's through the day. Two maps a kilometre round the camera, a cell a texel, painted on another thread when the camera has gone 128 m or the clock a quarter-hour (`ClimateMaps`): the ground's (snow depth to 0.5 m, its wetness, rain now, frost) and the leaves', a layer a kind, how many leaves and how far turned, each for a plant early and late in its kind's year, its seed choosing between. A kind's year is data (`assets/data/plant_years.ron`, `terra_climate::LeafYear`): its leaves out by warmth, turned and fallen by chill counted once the day is shorter than its length (fully an hour on), how much of them turn and drop, and for grass how it browns in a drought; a plant counts up to a quarter faster or slower by its seed, so a stand turns over days and none is ever ahead before the count begins. `--date <month-day or day>` sets the clock to a day of an Earth calendar, as that share of the world's year after midwinter, or a day of the clock; the log says the climate at the camera when the maps are first painted. | Standard models: budburst by growing degree-days, leaf colouring by cold degree-days under a shortening day (Delpierre et al. 2009). Interpolating the day's past keeps snow from arriving at midnight; early and late pictures per kind let each plant be its own without a tick. Measured: a sample a few microseconds, a kept cell a day 1.8 µs; the maps painted afresh, 1024 cells folded over a year, in under a second on another thread, after that a few milliseconds there; nothing on the GPU yet. |
| D328 | Body temperature and the going in code | Tech | Agent | Decided | D153's first effects, as climate.md orders them. A body's heat balance (`terra_climate::body`, shared, D312): it makes 58 W a square metre a met (one at rest, three walking or working, six sprinting) and takes 12% of the sun on level ground and 35% of a fire's heat on its side, and loses from 34 °C skin through its clothing (0.155 m²K/W a clo, half of it soaked) and the air (radiation 4.7, convection the greater of 3.1 and 8.3 v^0.6, ISO 7730's). Shivering makes up 100 W/m², sweating 220; what they cannot pushes the body from 37 °C by 0.03 °C a W/m², and it drifts there exponentially over 300 s of play. 36 to 38 °C is comfortable; past 35 or 39.5, health falls 0.5 a second a degree and does not heal (`Vitals::expose`). `BodyHeat` on players and people (gameplay's `warmth`), from their `ClimateSample` and their gear's (a person's outfit's) `warmth` in clo (`items.ron`: leather coat 1, cap 0.15; a body under its gear 0.1); rain soaks at a 120th a second a mm/h, snow half that, and dries over 240 s, faster by a fire; wading soaks at once. It says when it crosses an edge, in words, with no meter (D93). A person out of the band works a station slower (`body::pace`): half as fast at the edge of danger, never under a third (D169). A burning fire gives off 5 kW as `Heat`; a body in a room's probe is `Sheltered`. A walker's pace is multiplied by 1/(1 + snow/0.3 m) and up to a sixth off for wet ground above 0.7 that is not frozen (`body::going`), never below 0.35. | Standard thermal comfort and heat balance models, so cold, wind, wet and clothing do what players expect; numbers checked against known cases (a coat and a fire hold a winter's night, a bare body does not; resting in the shade of a 36 °C noon is bearable, hard work in its sun is not). The drift is five minutes so a night out is felt and survivable to act on, not instant (D134's half-hour day). In the game on 15 January at 03:00, bare, the player was cold in 20 s and freezing in 33. |
| D171 | Coming back from death | Design | Agent | Decided | A soul that dies falls into the astral and is lost, unless bound to a hearth by the rite adventurers learn when they rest at one they keep. The tie draws the soul back and the hearth's fire gives it a body made of magic. What you wore comes back with you; what you carried lies where you fell (D84); a little progress toward your next step goes into the new body (D101). You return to the hearth you last rested at in a claim, or a settlement's common hearth. Ordinary people die for good. | Left to me by the developers on 2026-10-12: "I don't care, you can come up with something meaningful here". It ties the death costs already decided to the lore, and makes a home where you come back to. See [lore.md](lore.md#coming-back-from-death). |

## Items and crafting

Equipment, packs, tools, stations and icons.

| # | Topic | Owner | By | Status | Decision | Why, or what closes it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D64 | Equipment | Design | Developers | Decided | An equipment grid, a character sheet: slots for what the player wears and carries as gear. The standard slots: head, chest (which covers the arms), gloves, legs, feet, back and belt; more may be added later. Not every slot shows on the body: some belt items do, such as a quiver, and some don't. No layers for now: one piece per slot. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10; the slots and no layers on 2026-10-12. Jewellery is open: perhaps two rings and a necklace, or instead a few trinket slots, probably not shown. See [items.md](items.md). |
| D66 | Action bar | Design | Developers | Decided | Items are put on an action bar to be used; whatever is in the picked slot is in hand. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10. Built in the game on `terra_items` (D83). |
| D83 | Items in code | Tech | Agent | Decided | The rules for items and containers live in an engine-free crate, `terra_items`. A container is a component on the entity that holds it; the items inside are data in that component, each with an identity, not entities of their own; an item loose in the world is an entity. Every change is an action the server checks with the same function the client uses to predict it. See [items.md](items.md). | Mine to decide. Inventories are state every player must agree on, so they stay out of Bevy (D28). Items as data inside a container keep saving and sending a player's inventory one small piece of data, and keep D58: what is in the world is an entity, and what you carry is state of you. Built and in use since 2026-10-10; the game's inventory has run on it since 2026-10-11. |
| D84 | Inventory baseline | Design | Developers | Decided | Subnautica, for now: a backpack grid where items take up shape and nothing stacks, equipment slots by kind, an action bar that points into the backpack, storage as more grids, and no weight. What you carry drops where you go down, and dropped items decay and disappear after a while. See [items.md](items.md). | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10: "lets say the baseline is just subnautica for now", and that weight and the like may be revisited. |
| D96 | Tools | Design | Developers | Decided | No science-fiction toolset, and no single tool that harvests everything: each job has its own tool. Whether items wear and need repair is open. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11. Replaces the excavator, harvester and scanner proposed earlier. A proposed set (axe, pickaxe, shovel, knife, hammer), and a recommendation on wear and repair, are in [crafting.md](crafting.md#tools). The game has axes, pickaxes, a knife and a hammer. |
| D99 | Wear and repair | Design | Developers | Decided | For now: tools, weapons and armour wear with use; a broken one stops working but is never lost; repair is cheap at a station in a claim, quicker with a recruit who knows the craft. | Agreed by the developers on 2026-10-11 as recommended, "for now". After Valheim. See [crafting.md](crafting.md#wear-and-repair). |
| D106 | Packs | Design | Developers | Decided | A pack is a container: an item worn in the pack slot that holds the items in it, and a bigger pack holds more. Take it off and what is in it stays in it. With no pack you carry nothing but what is in your hands, perhaps two hand slots (Leaning). | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "a pack IS a container that holds things, a bigger pack holds more things, thats what i mean. if you have no pack, you cant carry anything other than maybe we give you like two hands or something". Floated on 2026-10-11 after Stationeers; replaces D65. Fits death dropping what you carried (D84, D171). See [items.md](items.md#packs). Not built: the game still grows one backpack. |
| D109 | Crafting at stations | Design | Developers | Decided | Making takes time. You click a recipe, without holding, and it joins the station's queue, one per click; what it takes is paid when queued and given back if cancelled, after Enshrouded. Most stations work only while you stay at them. Some, such as a smelter, work through their queue on their own, and what they make waits in them. A station's screen is like the build menu: tiles of the recipes you have learned. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11, answering the questions in [crafting.md](crafting.md#the-station-screen). Paying on queueing and the screen's layout are from the proposal, which they accepted. Fuel for stations that work on their own is open. |
| D118 | Ammunition in a quiver | Design | Developers | Decided | A quiver is a container holding a stack of arrows, worn on the belt in a slot of its own. Arrows stack inside it; the quiver is one item. How many it holds is open. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11, answering the question in [items.md](items.md): "a quiver + stack of arrows in it.. a quiver is basically a container". An exception to nothing stacking (D84), inside a container. |
| D129 | How item icons are made | Tech | Agent | Decided | Icons are image files baked during development and shipped with the art; the game loads them, and an item with none shows its name. `cargo xtask icons` renders every item from its own look in one house style, sized to its cells: the fallback icon and the underdrawing for painting. `tools/icons/paint.ts` paints an item's icon from its render (or, with no look, from a description in `tools/icons/icons.txt`) with the image model, cuts it out and fits it. A manifest records the look and style each came from; a test fails when one is stale; the baker never replaces a painted icon. Variants, when items carry them, are shown on top of the icon (palette shift, frame, badge), not redrawn. See [items.md](items.md#item-icons) and the [item-icons playbook](../.agents/skills/item-icons/SKILL.md). | First built rendering icons in play; the developers doubted it, rightly: most games bake icons, and games with many generated items author one per kind and show variation on top. Rendering from the look stays as the underdrawing, so every icon is composed alike and matches its thing (D93), with no artist (D1). Painting reads far better at slot size. Research in [research/item-icons.md](../research/item-icons.md). |
| D130 | What item icons look like | Design | Agent | Leaning | In the art style (D14, now direction D, [art-style.md](art-style.md)); every icon is still painted in direction C (hand-painted surfaces, chunky readable forms, slightly exaggerated proportions), to be repainted in D once a D style reference is approved. A bold silhouette, warm light from the upper left, no background. Composed by the render: held things along the icon's diagonal, square on, working end up and to the right; everything else from the front, 15 degrees above, worn things as worn with no body in them. See [items.md](items.md#item-icons). | The developers wanted icons in the art style and left the rest to me. Renders were compared five ways, then painted icons against renders: painted read clearly better at slot size and held one style, so every item was painted on 2026-10-12. Leaning until the developers have looked at the full set. |
| D151 | Expanding a station | Design | Developers | Decided | Stations are expanded and upgraded, and their capabilities vary by how: an expansion may open new things to make, raise capacity, or improve speed, quality and other attributes. Not a fixed item per expansion: variations, which look different and organic. Expansions count anywhere in the station's building (D155). Proposed for the rest: an expansion is a role a station names (a forge's bellows, grindstone, tool rack), filled by any of several variations, each saying as data what it adds; a station outside any building counts what is within a generous reach; suggested places that snap, no visible slots, and the station's own look grows with what it has. | Asked for by the developers on 2026-10-12: "workstations should be expandable… I dont want fixed items for expansions, there should probably be variations or similar?" Then: "crafting stations will vary in capabilities when they expand/upgrade, and we want things to visually look different but also organic… it might unlock new craftables, improve capacit or other attributes, etc". On placing, between Soulmask's slots and Valheim's radius: "valheim gives you greater flexibility so maybe its a better approach (the one negative of valheim is the radius was always too tight)", then settled by D155: "lets make them count anywhere in the building". See [crafting.md](crafting.md#expanding-a-station). |

## Lore, magic, combat and growth

Essences, ranks, skills, fighting, creatures and threats.

| # | Topic | Owner | By | Status | Decision | Why, or what closes it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D68 | Research | Design | Developers | Decided | A research system unlocks technology and things to craft and build. How something is researched, and how a node is unlocked, is open. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10. Options and questions in [progression.md](progression.md). |
| D88 | Player skills | Design | Developers | Decided | There is a player progress system with a lot of skills, so there is always a sense of progression, and new skills can be picked up. Doing a thing gives you its skill automatically, and doing it more improves it. Other ways of gaining skills are likely but not chosen. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10. Much of it "could be" inspired by *He Who Fights With Monsters*, which is Leaning. Proposal and questions in [progression.md](progression.md#player-skills). |
| D97 | Combat and view | Design | Developers | Decided | Combat is important. The game is played in third person; first person may be an option later. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11. Proposal in [combat.md](combat.md). |
| D98 | Essences | Design | Developers | Decided | The books' system of essences, abilities and ranks is the baseline and a guide, tweaked where the game needs. How it plays is D163. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11 ("probably useful, but we may tweak it"), then built on by Decided items: the books' mechanics used as they are (D125), their rank names (D159), attributes (D164), cores (D165), spirit coins (D167) and quintessence (D168). See [magic.md](magic.md). |
| D100 | Taming and mounts | Design | Developers | Leaning | Animals can probably be tamed, and ridden as mounts. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11 ("we'll probably want"). How, and which, is open. |
| D102 | Mana | Design | Developers | Decided | There is mana as well as stamina: a pool of its own for abilities, beside health and stamina (D61). | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11. See [magic.md](magic.md#mana). |
| D103 | Where monsters come from | Design | Developers | Decided | Monsters form from pooled magic; there is ordinary wildlife besides. Raids on claims are not a concern for now. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11. See [creatures.md](creatures.md). |
| D117 | Archery | Design | Developers | Decided | Archery is a skill of its own, grown by shooting (D88). Each style of bow is probably a skill too (Leaning). | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11: archery is a skill plainly, the style of bow "probably". Proposed in [combat.md](combat.md): a style builds on archery rather than starting from nothing. Whether ammunition stacks is D118. |
| D119 | Hitboxes and critical hits | Design | Developers | Decided | Combat has hitboxes: where a blow lands matters, with headshots and critical hits. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11: "we're gonna want hitboxes/headshots/critical hit type scenarios". How it works is proposed in [combat.md](combat.md). |
| D124 | Where growth comes from | Design | Developers | Decided | Magic and special abilities come from essences (D98). Everything else comes from other sources: skills from doing them (D88), and techniques from books and practice, such as combat styles and disciplines of working with magic like astral magic. Which styles and disciplines there are is ours to invent (D125); how they are learned is D163. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11, of the books, and assumed by D125's styles and disciplines. See [progression.md](progression.md#three-layers). |
| D126 | Crossing a rank | Design | Developers | Leaning | Advancing to the next rank may need more than practice, such as a book or someone to help unlock it. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11, unsure. Proposed in [progression.md](progression.md#crossing-to-the-next-rank): steps from doing, and a key to cross a rank: a book, a teacher or a feat. |
| D132 | How damage shows | Design | Developers | Leaning | Damage shows on what was hit, as wounds and how it moves, not as numbers. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "damage should be visible on targets ideally, not numerical. like you should see the target get wounded", perhaps as a future thing. How it could work on every model is proposed in [combat.md](combat.md). The tech demo has no damage numbers; a blow sprays and the body jerks. |
| D156 | Gods | Design | Developers | Decided | There are gods, because they enrich the world. A god is a superior user of magic, one who went far past the highest rank a person reaches, and belongs to one realm, its nature following that realm's magic; no god is everywhere. In play, kept simple for now: blessings at shrines and thematic touches (shrines, priests, holy places); more later. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "let's keep gods as they just enrich the world. They're just like 'superior magic users' I think, and they're realm specific". See [lore.md](lore.md#gods). |
| D157 | Beings of the gap between realms | Design | Developers | Leaning | Something older and larger than gods lives in the gap between realms, perhaps keeping the cosmos in order, known only in the lore and never met in play, for now. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12, of the books' great cosmic beings: "could also be ok but maybe they're just in lore?" See [lore.md](lore.md#beings-of-the-gap). |
| D158 | Names and generated lore | Design | Agent | Leaning | Proposed in [naming.md](naming.md): the books' names for the core systems (D125), and for everything ours plain English with no metaphor (the water metaphor was dropped on 2026-10-12 as odd), and a generated tongue for each realm's peoples, its sound picked by the realm's magic; places named twice, in their people's tongue and plainly in English; lore generated by one chain from the realm's magic to what is left in its land. The docs keep their working terms until it is agreed. | Asked for by the developers on 2026-10-12: "come up with a thematic pattern that you can continue to generate names, lore, etc. from (like a documented set of rules or something)", so nothing is named in the books' words (D125). |
| D159 | Rank names | Design | Developers | Decided | The ranks are the books' own, low to high: iron, bronze, silver, gold, diamond, with normal rank below them for those without magic. The one place our names follow *He Who Fights With Monsters* exactly (D125). A rank is always said with "rank", so the metal alone means the material. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "I want the exact same rank names as from the books", replacing naming.md's proposal of Stream to Ocean. |
| D163 | The magic system | Design | — | Open | Proposed in [magic.md](magic.md), except the parts marked decided. There are four ways to have magic: essence powers, studied crafts, combat styles, and things that hold magic. Three essences and a confluence give five powers each, woken by awakening stones. A power is drawn by weighted chance from a generated pool, hundreds per essence, built the way shipped games generate loot (a base, affixes in families and tiers, tag weights, budgets, uniques) from a shared library of mechanics (blink, flight, shield, summon and so on). Each essence flavours a mechanic with its own look and side effect, and mechanics combine, so they overlap between characters while powers rarely do. Essences beyond the core list are generated per realm. The weights are shaped by the stone, your other essences, the powers and skills you already have, and where you are; a power's whole ladder is fixed when drawn. Decided on 2026-10-12: there is no separate tally of habits. There is one draw, with no choosing among offers, as the developers said the same day; proposed, a stone on an essence has a short list of outcomes, about five to ten, one drawn and then made individual by the loot machine's rolls. Decided on 2026-10-12: awakening is a ritual that shows what is known of the list (everything for a common stone on a common essence, little or nothing for rare and legendary ones) and what can't happen, never numbers or the result. Combat styles come in five forms, one skill book each. Crafts (ritual magic, wardcraft, astral magic, bindcraft, alchemy, inscription, enchanting) are learned from books by rank, with components used up, diagrams in the symbols of magic, chanted spells and backlash, as in the books. Old-school touches run throughout. | Asked for by the developers on 2026-10-12: "some nostalgia from old school magic, but we also need to remember our world design/lore", with examples for each system and procedural, random learning from essences shaped by what was learned before. Mechanics after the books (D98, [research/hwfwm-magic.md](../research/hwfwm-magic.md)); every essence, power, style and working ours (D125). |
| D164 | Attributes | Design | Developers | Decided | Four attributes, as in the books: Power, Speed, Spirit and Recovery. Each essence binds to one when absorbed; each has its own rank, and a person's rank is a function of them. Proposed in [magic.md](magic.md#attributes): they rise as the powers bound to them rise, and what each raises. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "keep the attributes yes". |
| D165 | Monster cores | Design | Developers | Decided | Monsters leave cores. Absorbing them hurries your least-advanced powers, at the books' price: each makes practice count for less, and leaning on them makes crossing past gold harder. Cores are also crafts' components, a way to calm pools, and a trade good. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "keep the cores". See [magic.md](magic.md#raising-powers). |
| D167 | Money is magic | Design | Developers | Decided | Money works as in the books: the currency is magic condensed and ranked, iron to diamond, left by monsters and formed where magic pools thickly. It is spent, burned as fuel for workings and items, used as a component, and absorbed above your rank for a short surge of strength that leaves you exhausted. The same in every realm. Named as in the books: spirit coins. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "I like the way money and stuff works in the book that can also be burned for other things". See [magic.md](magic.md#money-that-is-magic). |
| D168 | Fragments of essences | Design | Developers | Decided | Small fragments of one essence's kind, as the books' quintessence, left by monsters and pools of that kind: components for workings, enchanting and items of that kind. Not absorbable. Named as in the books: quintessence. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "yes". See [magic.md](magic.md#fragments-of-essences). |
| D172 | Threats, and an organisation across realms | Design | Developers | Decided | No single long threat: each realm's threats vary, from its conditions, monsters and events. Across realms, a dangerous organisation causes problems throughout your journey, perhaps behind some bosses. Proposed: essence users who force power by waking pools and feeding surges, a cell per realm generated with its leader, a few lieutenants who recur; a faction like any other (D95), not a fixed story. All are generated from a set of parts (what drives them, what they do, how far they reach, how big, who leads), and there may be many. **The Opened** are the worked example the parts are built from, after the books' cults that serve something beyond the world: they serve a being of the astral and feed surges to thin the walls between realms. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "normal world conditions, could be also monsters, could be other world events, but I think this could vary realm by realm? There should also be sort of anti-heroes or whatever that are a dangerous organization that causes problems throughout your journey (including maybe influencing bosses etc? idk)". Replaces premise B's spreading threat. The Opened chosen, and more than one allowed, on 2026-10-12: "ok A is fine. We may have more than one btw (procedural!!!!)"; then "we'd probably generate them all from a set of parts, but this is an example we'd build from". See [lore.md](lore.md#threats). |
| D173 | Knowledge of essences, and essence users among people | Design | Developers | Decided | What a world knows of what stones give grows as anyone in it awakens an outcome not yet known, and knowing ritual magic shows one or two outcomes beyond it. Leaning: some areas have people who hold essences, or special kinds of people who do; what they have is known locally. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "yeah I think so, especially because maybe NPCs will get essences in some areas", and of ritual magic showing more, "kind of nice idea because then it rewards you for progressing into other stuff". See [magic.md](magic.md#before-you-awaken-what-is-known), [people.md](people.md#essence-users). |
| D180 | Combat's baseline | Design | Developers | Decided | Enshrouded is the baseline for combat: fast-paced, fun, with good animations and smooth play. An action RPG, where a fight is weapon play, timing and footwork; not mashing a button to perform an ability. Abilities (D66) and combat styles (D124) add to that rather than replacing it. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "our baseline is enshrouded: its pretty fast paced, its fun, its got good animations and smooth gameplay. its not just like 'mash a button to perform an ability' ... so action-rpg esque". Firms up D85's liking for its combat. What Enshrouded does is in [research/enshrouded-combat.md](../research/enshrouded-combat.md); how ours would follow it is proposed in [combat.md](combat.md). |
| D250 | Worman, Drillhog and Seawish | Design | Developers | Decided | Monsters drawn by a developer's daughter, made in the game's style ([creatures.md](creatures.md#designed-kinds)): the Worman, a giant earthworm disguised as a traveller in a cap and dark glasses, with red lips and a knife, that attacks when you come close; and the Drillhog, a boar-sized groundhog with drills growing from its back and head, which it digs and attacks with, met in dungeons and underground; and the Seawish, a fish standing on its tail with seaweed growing on its head, fighting with a sword and its seaweed, met on beaches and hidden in the water as a clump of seaweed. Models exist; rigs, animations and behaviour are to come. | The developers, 2026-10-13: "These are the characters that are gonna be mobs or enemies that are designed by my daughter. Obviously, they have to fit the style of the game." 2026-10-14: "this is seawish and its a fish with seaweed in its head. you can imagine how it would disguise itself in water" |

## People and the tribe

NPCs, factions, claims, recruits and outposts.

| # | Topic | Owner | By | Status | Decision | Why, or what closes it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D70 | NPCs | Design | Developers | Decided | There are NPCs. What they are and do is open. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10. Questions in [gameplay.md](gameplay.md). |
| D73 | Agents as players | Design | Developers | Decided | The game can be played headless, by an agent alone, through the same command line and remote protocol as QA (D69); an MCP server only if a client needs one. The agent is a client like any other and keeps to the same rules. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-10. How it sees and acts is a proposal in [multiplayer.md](multiplayer.md#agents-as-players); whether it is for us or for players too is open. |
| D90 | Answering threats | Design | Developers | Decided | Part of survival is establishing claims and outposts and holding them (D166). Recruits help defend them (D161, D169); protecting an outpost, with walls, guards or otherwise, is part of having one. Built defences, automation, may come later. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11 ("I could imagine", "possible"), then settled by D161, D166 and D169 on 2026-10-12: recruits defend if you are attacked near your claim, and "part of having this tribe/village you build is you will need to actually protect it". Recruits come mostly by rescue or as a reward for helping a settlement. Replaces the automation half of D3. See [tribe.md](tribe.md#defence). |
| D94 | Claims | Design | Developers | Decided | You build within areas you claim, so the world is not destroyed everywhere and nobody has to manage it. A claim belongs to a tribe and is one of its outposts (D166); nothing regrows inside one by default (D150). How claims are placed, limited and kept up is proposed in [building.md](building.md#claims); their size is D104. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11 ("will likely need"), and assumed by every later decision on outposts, regrowth and buildings (D150, D155, D166, D171). Technically, it bounds the world's stored changes to what players hold (D47, D52). |
| D95 | Factions and settlements | Design | Developers | Decided | The world needs systems like Kenshi's for how it exists: villages with things going on, and factions. Factions have no fixed set or number: more are added over time, and players discover them, learning about each as they meet it. Settlements can be lost for good; systems can repopulate if needed. Players do not capture people or keep prisoners: it sets the wrong tone. Hostile settlements may hold prisoners of their own, whom players can rescue, which is one way to recruit (D90). Whether players can take over a settlement is open, to decide later. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11. Proposed in [gameplay.md](gameplay.md#factions-and-settlements-after-kenshi): factions as data, settlements with needs, people travelling, a world that remembers, standing with each faction, a gentler tone than Kenshi's. |
| D104 | Claim size | Design | Developers | Leaning | A claim starts at a reasonable size and can be upgraded; the numbers are adjustable. Proposed: 40 metres on a side, upgradable to 80 and 160 (Enshrouded's upgraded size). | The developers set the rule on 2026-10-11 and left the size to be chosen. |
| D123 | Recruits progress | Design | Developers | Decided | People who join you have their own progression: they get better, as you do. Whether other people in the world progress is open. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11: "npcs would also have their own internal progression system, at least ones that are recruited". How is proposed in [people.md](people.md#recruits). |
| D161 | The tribe: recruits who look after themselves | Design | Developers | Decided | You recruit people, bring them back safely, and they live in your tribe and take part in its work: working crafting stations and helping haul resources. It is not a colony sim: you do not micromanage them, and they are functional on their own. They have stats they level up as you do, so they get better at the work they do. Building help is carrying things to a building, and no one starts on a plan you are still designing; they should potentially help build too (Leaning). They take their gear on their own, from containers that are the tribe's; a container is either personal or the tribe's. Needs, and how they die, are D169. They can die. They will travel and fight beside you, later. No ageing for now; families are wishful thinking. Kenshi is the basis to some degree, and Medieval Dynasty a source of inspiration. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "we want to be able to recruit npcs … and have them 'live' in our tribe … the main delta is we dont want this to be a colony sim … they should automatically be functional … basically you'll have a little automatically managed tribe simulator". Answering the questions the same day: "building help is useful when you need to carry things to a building, but … you dont want an npc magically to start building your blueprint while you're still designing it"; "they'd take their gear on their own but its possible we'll need systems that help you decide what they can and cant use"; "families is neat, but thats more of a wishful thinking. we dont need age in the game … for now"; "recruits can die"; travelling with you is "a future TODO". Then: "recruits should help build potentially yes"; "maybe a personal chest is actually enough (or maybe you can configure how each storage container is used?)"; "needs we should document some ideas, but figure out later". Then, of containers: "personal vs tribe seems enough". Builds on D90 and D123. Proposed in [tribe.md](tribe.md); what other games do in [research/recruits-at-the-base.md](../research/recruits-at-the-base.md). |
| D162 | How recruits take work | Design | Developers | Decided | Recruits take part on their own, in whatever way the simulation makes sense; the player does not assign them. Proposed for how: the best-skilled takes each piece of work, then whoever likes it, then whoever is nearest; who does what is worked out again only when something changes; asking a recruit is the one override; those with nothing to do help carry and haul. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12, first as likely ("they likely will automatically 'claim' a job based on either some stats superiority or something else"), then: "i like the idea that you are able to recruit folks to your tribe/village, bring them back safely, and then they will automatically just participate in whatever way the simulation makes sense". Skills grown by doing risk locking a recruit into the first work they took, which liking is proposed to counter ([tribe.md](tribe.md#who-does-what)). |
| D166 | Tribes and outposts | Design | Developers | Decided | A tribe is its players and recruits: you can recruit other players too. A tribe holds its claims, each an outpost, and all its outposts are shared within it. Recruits are not shared between outposts: each lives and works at one; whether they travel between them is to be decided later. A player is in one tribe at a time. Tribes are like clans or guilds, with two ranks, member and leader: members build, use the stores and recruit; leaders also invite, remove and rank players, place and give up claims, and take down anything. A player on their own is a tribe of one. Moving a recruit to another outpost is likely done at a station, for now the claim stone, and you take them with you or let them walk on their own at the risk of their death (Leaning). | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "i think a tribe belongs to the claim, and everyone in the tribe (including other players, as you can recruit them too) are part of it". Then: "one tribe across all claims i think, but each 'claim' is like an outpost … you might need different npcs at different ones? or they'd have to travel between them … for now, tribes, and outposts, and all outposts are shared amongst one tribe, but npcs will be not be shared amongst outposts"; "players can only be in one tribe, and you'll have tribes kind of like clans/guilds so at least two ranks". Then: the proposed split of ranks and a tribe of one were agreed ("yep thats fine", "that seems fine"); "moving recruits between outposts will be some kind of station you use to do something, and you probably have to take them with you, or let them walk on their own and risk death?"; then, of the station: "it can be the claim stone for now?" Gives D94's "claims belong to someone, a player or a group" its group. See [tribe.md](tribe.md#tribes-and-outposts). |
| D169 | Recruits' bodies, needs and death | Design | Developers | Decided | Recruits are generally much like a player: health, stamina and mana, hunger, thirst and body temperature. Cold or heat out of comfort slows their work. They sleep at night and need a bed, which they claim on their own, as they do work; without one, their stamina stays very low, so they work very slowly all the time; potentially they sleep on the ground, badly rested, getting a little stamina back (Leaning). They die when their health runs out; they also die of hunger and thirst, but slowly, so it is a hard mistake to make. A way to revive them may come later. When one dies, what they had drops where they fell; graves may come later. What recruits forage for themselves is poor food; cooked food is better, filling more hunger, and may give other boosts. Monsters likely do not form inside an outpost, but danger needs to be close to home: protecting the outpost, with walls, guards or otherwise, is part of having one. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12: "they'll need basic survival things like food/water … it'd be nice if they had to sleep? could be they just sleep at night … they'll need to deal w/ temperature as well as itll slow down their work … stamina might actually be useful (along with mana) if we need them in combat. i guess npcs generally should be pretty similar to a player"; "they'd die from not having health. maybe we'll allow a way to revive them in the future … if we 'spawn monsters' we likely wont spawn them 'in our town' and part of having this tribe/village you build is you will need to actually protect it … they SHOULD also die of starvation and thirst but it should be protracted and a hard mistake to make". Then: hunger and thirst, once empty, "slowly eat away at health over days, not crazy fast", weakening them; foraged food might be poor and cooked food better, unsure, then: "yeah thats my thinking, it'd basically give you more hunger and maybe other boosts"; graves "could be a feature addition later, for now they could just drop their items". Then: "lets just say sleep at night for now is the plan. npcs will need a bed. if they dont have one they're going to have basically no stamina which means they'll work very slow continuously", and "beds they can auto claim or something? similar to how the tribe itself auto sims"; then "we could let npcs sleep on the ground potentially … they wouldn't be very well rested but it could let them reclaim a tiny bit of stamina". Replaces the recommendation that recruits are downed before dead. See [tribe.md](tribe.md#needs). |
