| D257 | Auditing building, and the holes it closed | Tech | Agent | Decided | `cargo xtask rules` audits building end to end ([research/building-audit.md](../research/building-audit.md)): `terra_build`'s rules tests (`tests/rules.rs`; a rule known to fail is `#[ignore]`d with its reason, so `cargo xtask check` gates what holds and the audit reports the rest), the random operations over 64 seeds, a drawing audit of every sort of village building in every kit with its dress (`examples/drawing.rs`: laid twice the same, coplanar overlaps that would z-fight, what one edit sets waiting and merges, an operation's cost), and the rules checked live (`tools/sandbox/rules.ts`), with `Build: change on screen` logged when a change's sections are all on screen and `sandbox.frames` giving a stretch of frames' spread. What it found and closed: a walker is stopped where an underside overhead comes lower within a column than at its edge, as an inverted slope's does, not only at the column's edge (`Base::walk` sweeps the move); a door is not shut on someone standing in it; something set down (a workbench, the claim stone) is in the way of a cube built through it, as a body is (construction-001, gap 17); and the pickaxe does not dig within a scoop of any column a build stands on or has planned (D193). | Mine, on 2026-10-13, asked by the developers to "make sure we're still on track for making the voxels work correctly and efficiently, and look good. and all of our physics rules/etc are working"; checks only a picture would show become a tool (D71). |
| D287 | Building's junk checked by meshes and by frames, and ratcheted | Tech | Agent | Decided | What a renderer would draw as junk is checked without a person, two ways ([docs/building-drawing.md](building-drawing.md)). From the meshes, with no GPU, `cargo xtask artifacts` (`terra_build::inspect`) counts faults of each kind in each kit's bases (not finite, black, inward, coplanar, doubled, see-through, under the roof, poking out, buried under a surface's relief) against a checked-in baseline, `crates/terra_build/examples/artifacts-baseline.tsv`: `cargo xtask check` ratchets each kit's and each two kits' rectangle under a gable on every commit and fails when any count rises; `--record` lowers it as fixes land. From frames on the GPU, at the high and the low preset, `cargo xtask artifacts --frames` holds still views (acne, crawl, mip shimmer), the sun moving (acne, cascades), a glide down a street (a pixel flipping for a frame) and a house going up outside and in (flashes, TAA trails, the picture's brightness jumping) to limits. A room's light probe fades in and out over 0.8 s as the room closes or opens, as engines blend a probe's weight. | The developers on 2026-10-13: "make sure we got no flicker or other junk rendering issues"; the third goals review: 31,710 faults and nothing gating on them. The frames found one: a room's probe dimmed it by two fifths in one frame as its last roof cube went in; the rest were clean at both presets, and stayed so with the sun's shadow biases taken to nothing. |
| D290 | One way to draw every construction material, for hundreds of materials | Design | Developers | Decided | As [construction-002.md](proposals/construction-002.md) says. The goal is the developers': one way for every material, simple to add, hundreds of them, performant. The method is the agent's: every material, wall, floor or roof, is one kind of data drawn by one engine in four patterns (block, coursed, roof, frame), each owning corners, joins, openings, gables, ghosts, part-built, detail by distance and swaps. A material is a small file (its id, pattern, reference pack, cost, weight and overrides), a material instance merged over its pattern's file and its pack (`assets/data/kits/patterns/`, `assets/data/packs/`); pieces are shared per pattern and wear the material's bake, a material's own pieces an optional override; one command (`cargo xtask pack`) makes a pack from a CC0 scan, a Tripo reference or a generator, checks and credits it and registers the material. Each role either wears the bake on geometry the rules shape or places a reference piece whole where its outline is the look, chosen per pattern by visual merit (block: profiles worn, corner stones placed; coursed: members worn, the modelled log an override; roof: covering worn, ridges, verges and eaves placed; frame: members worn). Drawn from texture arrays with a layer per vertex, one draw per pattern per section whatever the number of materials, a texture budget a material, only the layers laid sections use resident on the GPU, and a swap where solidity does not change a lookup of layers. Amends D177 and D251 (rule-shaped geometry wearing a hand-made bake is allowed), D197, D200, D215 and D258 (their kit files become material files over patterns and packs). How long a change of material takes the player stays D232. No code is customised by material (the developers: "thats why its important that these kits are cleanly designed so each one doesnt have to be customized by the game"): a test fails on a material's or pack's name or a kind by number in `terra_build` or `terra_game`, and a settlement names its materials in data. Every placed piece keeps D303's contract: real size (`dims`) from its origin at its anchor, seated by the pattern's rule on a roof's deck or covering, `start` and `end` sockets, one model one size wherever used (the kit checker); a pattern's pieces are shared (`Trim`), a material's own override them (`Piece`). | The developers on 2026-10-14: "my goal is to have uniformity. one way we do these construction materials (or maybe one per type, floors roof, etc) … they need to be efficient when placing materials to quickly swap to the right visual"; "can each material be abstracted into like effectively data files or are we having to micro manage a bunch of complex things?"; "what im aiming for, again, is it being pretty straight forward.. and simple, to add new materials, that way we could have 100s of them. models are fine, generated is fine, whatever is going to make them visually and technically work the best. it also has to be performant". The evidence ([research/uniform-materials.md](../research/uniform-materials.md)): the Tripo log's texture baked onto the cubes' surface read as logs from the street and as a striped box at a corner; the same bark baked onto the generated logs came most of the way to the modelled log and kept their ends and corners, lacking only its outline, stubs and girth; roof coverings already are surfaces and hold; a swap took 110 to 145 ms to the screen in every candidate, most of it laying the base again. |
| D298 | A change of material within a pattern is a repaint at once | Design | Agent | Decided | Amends D232. Change material between two kinds of one family (one pattern, framed or not) is a repaint at once: the change's every step, the old taken out and given back, the swap and the new put in, done as one act and checked as one (`plans::change`), so what stands changes its material where it is, as Satisfactory's Customizer and V Rising's wallpaper repaint; across families, where the structure itself changes, the cubes are marked and built anew a step at a time as D232 has it. Drawn as a lookup (D290): a change of material and nothing else rewrites the layers of the changed cubes' corners in the meshes drawn, on screen the next frame, the sections laid again behind it with the new kind's weathering and joints; the material in a hammer is kept resident so the lookup never waits for it. | Construction 002's third question, left to the agent with every open call on 2026-10-14 ("Like get it done"); the developers' "they need to be efficient when placing materials to quickly swap to the right visual". Measured on the Windows build: the lookup 0.5 ms of work and on screen the frame after the change, the sections laid again three frames later. |
| D349 | A material's pack checked as it is added | Tech | Agent | Decided | D290's known failure modes, the inputs a material made from a scan brings that the drawing would take without a word, fail loudly when the material is added (`cargo xtask pack`, which then keeps nothing of it) and on every check (`cargo xtask kits`), each saying what is wrong and how to fix it (`terra_build::packs`): its height map weak, inverted (`--invert-height`), noise or not its colour map's, judged by its correlation with its occlusion or colour; a texture that does not tile, its edges' step against the largest inside it; its real size off its pattern's range, stones 10 to 60 cm and flags 15 to 90 cm counted from its height's cells, logs 20 to 40 cm with their bark laid at their girth, a covering's courses 12 to 70 cm; relief and parallax past what its pattern draws well; a role its pattern asks every pack for (block and frame: walls, floors; coursed: face, daub; roof: covering, rafters, its own pieces) left to the pattern's default rather than given by the pack or the pack it is laid over; glossy or metal maps, or a reflectance out of 0.05 to 0.6; a field its pattern never reads (a roof's `timber`, a block or frame kind's daub colour), named with the file that sets it (`packs::fields`). Each limit sits past the worst shipped scan and short of planted bad ones, both tested. Each new material gets a preview sheet to look over once (`tools/kits/preview.ts`): the kit sheet lays its preview alone west of the start, every role and shape of its pattern, pictured at 10 m and 2 m at noon and in the evening; `cargo xtask pack` makes it where a GPU draws the game and prints its command elsewhere. [construction-002](proposals/construction-002.md#what-each-pattern-can-and-cannot-express) lists what each pattern can and cannot express, so a material needing a new pattern (code) is known before it is made. | The developers on D290: "what i dont want is 'why does this material look fucked up? oh the system doesnt handle it well because it has this thing we decided on'… i just wanna make sure this is the right design". The design is the industry's (material arrays, trim sheets, shared pieces); its failures are bad inputs, so the inputs are what is checked. |
| D258 | One material system, built: surfaces, modules and stones | Tech | Agent | Decided | D251 built as [building-plan.md](building-plan.md#one-material-system-d251-d258) says: a kind is its material's textures, its pieces by role and its pattern. Pieces are profiles swept along edges, modules (a length of mesh repeated along a line, in whole copies stretched to fit, on a plane or draped over a crease, capped at its ends) and stones set at points, written into the kit's pieces file by `tools/kits`. As first built, every piece was made by a script in Blender from numbers in `art/kits/<kit>/pieces.json`: not modelled, so not yet the hand-made models D251 asks for (goals review 2026-10-14, finding 1). Modelled pieces, from Tripo and cleaned up in Blender, textured in their own UVs, replace the scripted ones role by role, each kept in `art/kits/<kit>/` and read by `tools/kits` in place of numbers; code only places, cuts to length and fits them. Their concepts and models were written and judged by the agent; the nine on the approval sheet (thatch's ridge, verge and eave, shakes' ridge cap, the weathered board, the board valley, slate's ridge tiles, wattle's oak member and the whole log) are accepted as D290's shared reference pieces, the call left to the agent on 2026-10-14 ("Like get it done"). Which pieces are modelled so far is building-plan.md's to say: since 2026-10-14 every roof's ridge, hip, verge and eave, shakes' and slate's valleys and flashing, and wattle's frame, each baked from its model into its kit's atlas (`Piece`). A roof's covering is its cubes' sloped surface in the covering's material, with thickness and relief from its height map, its rows on level lines; its ridges, hips, valleys, eaves, verges, apexes and flashing are its kit's modules and stones, and its rafters, ridge beam and battens modules too (amends D241: no generated courses, caps or boards). A framed kind's members are modules laid by `layout::frames`'s rule (amends D236). Each role a kind dresses takes a piece of its own materials (D215, extended to roofs and frames). Far detail from each module's far mesh. Logs stay generated (D200) and can take a module when the developers choose. Amended by D290: modules and stones are the pattern's, a material's own an override in its pack. | Mine, building D251 on 2026-10-13: the developers asked for "hand made models ... we are doing what enshrouded does", and Enshrouded's roof galleries show exactly this: a displaced material surface with modelled ridge, verge and eave pieces. Their screenshot of a thatch gable showed what generated coverings gave: a half-disc ridge with sky under it, thin flat verge sheets with a sawtooth, a sawn log end at the apex. |
| D260 | Parts that touch and agree are one | Tech | Agent | Decided | Amends D199's parts: a part groups cubes for the interface, the order they go in and their refunds, so cubes laid by hand (*Block*, a brush's box) join the part of the cubes of their kind beside them laid on the same terms, built or planned, and every such part they touch is merged into it; taking cubes out of the plan splits a part they leave in pieces, the piece with the first cube keeping its number (`terra_build::parts`). The moves are `Op::Regroup`, checked and undone like any other, a new part taking the old one's terms, so refunds stay exact. Taking apart a planned cube of a part partly built takes it out of the plan. The room, wall and roof tools still lay a part each. | Mine, from the goals review's finding 3 on 2026-10-13 ([goals-review-2026-10-13.md](../research/goals-review-2026-10-13.md)): a part a click filled a section's palette of 255 long before its 4,096 cubes, and the hammer refused ("Too many parts meet here"), which blocked D226. Merging only parts that agree keeps refunds exact without a cost per cube; a property test lays thousands of cubes by hand across sections with no refusal (`ops/tests/by_hand.rs`). |
| D261 | Rooms, doorways and door steps from the cubes alone | Tech | Agent | Decided | Rooms (D155) are the space the built cubes close in all round, floor and roof too, found by flooding in three dimensions the cubes not built that have something built over and under them in their column, as Minecraft's and Enshrouded's room checks do; a flood that reaches the sky or the open ground is the outside. Only what is built closes a room in; an opening is part of its wall. So a room is any height and shape (two storeys and a mezzanine are one room), a knee-high ring of wall is none, and every room is indoors; they are worked out when asked after a change (`terra_build::rooms`), from the last rooms found where there are some: only the rooms beside a cube whose closing in changed are let go and flooded again, the rest kept, so a castle edit costs its rooms near it, not the castle (D308). A doorway is any opening at a floor: an open cube with something not open under it, two cubes clear, a floor on one side at the threshold's layer leading, through the rest of the opening, into a room and not into the wall (a window in a thick wall is none), and none on the other (`Base::doorways`), however and on whatever storey it was made. Door steps are cubes (amends D206): from each doorway out, a stair a column rising to the threshold, a quarter of a metre a riser, the first a half cube where one riser is all the drop needs, the last a whole cube where the ground rises over a stair's lower tread (on a slope falling from the door, a stair there showed a thin wedge over the ground), each on a footing to the cube the ground passes through, of the footings' kind, as footings take the ground; none where the ground is within 20 cm, none for a door more than four metres over it (`Base::door_steps`). The hammer and the remote plan them as a part of their own when an opening or a carving makes a doorway; the shortcuts lay them built with their footings. Nothing outside the cubes collides, and the planet is no longer read for them at run time. | Mine, from the goals review's findings 4 and 5 on 2026-10-13 ([goals-review-2026-10-13.md](../research/goals-review-2026-10-13.md)): door steps collided outside any cube and took their height from the planet at run time, against D192's "the cube is the truth"; rooms were found per six-cube storey in 2D, so walls built off a storey's layer or storeys over a roof were misread, and doorways needed a door-shaped wall tile on the ground storey. Stairs and halves exist since D220 and D231, as D206 waited for. |
| D265 | Claims keep ten metres apart, and clear of villages | Tech | Agent | Decided | D193's "claims do not overlap, keep a margin" enforced: a claim stone is refused, and a claim does not grow, where its edge would come within 10 m of another claim's edge, or of anywhere a village reaches (along its street, and each building's door, back and corners), measured across the ground (`play::claim::too_near`). The hammer says why. | Mine, on 2026-10-14, as the lead asked: D193 said it and nothing enforced it, between claims or against the village. Ten metres leaves room to walk round a base and keeps what stands out at a claim's edge, eaves and footings, off a neighbour's; growth is checked too, as a claim that grows into another overlaps as surely as one placed there. |
| D277 | Wall or floor, read from the cubes | Tech | Agent | Decided | Whether a cube is in a wall is read from the cubes round it, not from storeys of six (`Base::in_wall`): a cube not of a roof is in a wall when a whole cube, a post or an opening, not of a roof, is over it or under it; one with nothing under it is a floor where a cube of floor, with nothing over it or under it, is beside it or corner to corner with it, as a floor runs on under the foot of a wall standing on it; one alone in its column is a wall's first course where it rests on the ground and is in no slab two by two on its layer, and a walkway in the air (2026-10-15); a stair or slope set on a floor leaves it a floor. Which way a wall runs is read the same way (`Base::runs_along`). The wall tiles the base kept are gone: a storey is only the quick tools' default height, the room and wall tools reading a column of six cubes as a tile (`Base::filling`, worked out when asked). | Mine, from the goals review's finding 5 on 2026-10-14, as the lead asked: walls built by hand off a storey's layer, or storeys over a roof (D226), were read by the storey they fell in. The rule keeps what every kit draws as it was (the layout tests are unchanged), and reads a wall from layer 3 to 8 as one wall; a timber walkway a cube wide is a deck at any layer, not a log wall at one and a deck at a storey's floor (the second review's finding 6), and the plain mesh's colours read rooms by the cube. Wattle's frame is modules since D258, but `layout::frames` still puts its rails and girts at multiples of a storey (`STOREY_CUBES`), until that rule reads the cubes too. |
| D280 | Villages and scale scenes put up by the operations | Tech | Agent | Decided | As D108 asks, a village's buildings, the town and castle the scale benches draw, and the bench village are put up by the checked operations a player's are (`Base::put_up`, `Build::put_up`): the shortcuts still say what each building is, then it is laid as plans, a part a kind, its openings opened, and built a layer at a time from the bottom, every step checked (nothing floats, built from the bottom, a roof on what holds it), in its kits' terms; a refusal is logged and the shortcuts' base kept. A test puts up every building of the village, a town and the castle and finds the same cubes. Laying the shortcuts' result through the operations, rather than rewriting each plan as operations, keeps the variety (D229) and makes every rule a check on it. |
| D279 | No open sky through the leaves of a forest, and its haze green in a glade | Tech | Agent | Decided | Leaves take 0.05 of Bevy's own diffuse transmission (it was 0.15 to 0.35): it lights each leaf's far side from the open sky's environment map, unshaded by the crowns, and the light through a leaf is the foliage shader's own, from the sun and from the sky occlusion volume (D247, D272). The forest's haze stays its own until the sky over the eye is more than half open (it gave way from 0.4, D273), and under the trees is two thirds the green of light come through leaves rather than a third, the rest the horizon's. | Review of 2026-10-14: the middle distance among the veteran oaks "turns teal-grey", blue above green. Measured at the walk's 30 s: blue 0.334 against green 0.351 there, 0.310 against 0.322 after, red down more; with the volume's own light zeroed it was bluer still, so the blue came from what ignores the canopy. |
| D284 | A block surface's triangles only where its shape bends | Tech | Agent | Decided | How D197's surface is meshed, as terrain and voxel renderers mesh theirs: a cube's face has points only where its shape bends, across the rounding of each edge open to the air (three steps near, two a few metres off, one far), and is otherwise one flat square; earth's packing, real relief in its shape, keeps a point every quarter cube near. The stone's relief is no longer displaced vertices but its maps': a normal map and parallax at the texture's own depth (`depth` in each kit's textures: stone 0.025, brick 0.015, wattle's daub 0.02, earth and lime-wash as they were), flat at the low preset (D239). A cube cut to a roof or a shape has points also where its cut crosses its top or foot or bends, all a straight cut needs (earth's, packed, keeps its even steps); one with a lintel or sill bedded within reach has one every 10 cm near, for the wall's lap over the piece. Where faces of different detail meet, each side's line takes the points of every cube round it (`kin`: the cubes drawn into the same surface, in this call or another), so the surface stays closed with no T-junctions; a cell given points by a neighbour is fanned from its middle. Normals are the rounding's own where the surface is no more than rounded. A round log's bark likewise: its relief is its material's parallax at the timber's `relief` (`Kit::depth`), not its points, and its rings come only where its shape bends, every 25 cm near (`timber::SHAPE_RING_M`) and three across each knot, a metre apart a few metres off with no knots, every 25 cm still where a log is cut to a roof's slope. The budget near (`surface::NEAR_TRIANGLES_A_SQUARE_METRE`, `sections::NEAR_TRIANGLES_A_CUBE` and `NEAR_MIB_A_BASE`): 64 triangles a square metre of surface open to the air, 120 a cube with its dress and 4 MiB a building, held by tests on a wall and a stone house in `cargo xtask check` and on the village by `cargo xtask sections`. | Asked for by the lead on 2026-10-14: "Fix the block surface's density the way shipped voxel and terrain renderers do", "Fine stone relief from the normal and height maps ... not from displaced vertices", "It must look the same or better at 2, 5 and 10 m". A face was ten by ten squares, 200 triangles, at every distance under 25 m; the village's near detail fell from 14.2 to 10.8 million triangles, the block kinds' surface and dress to 109 a cube, a stone house's surface from 800 triangles a square metre (200 a face) to under 64; stone, earth and brick side by side at 2, 5 and 10 m look the same ([research/building-scale.md](../research/building-scale.md)). Amends how D197 draws its relief, not what it looks like. |
| D278 | Bases far off: a hull, merged by cell to the horizon, and streamed by distance | Tech | Agent | Decided | Amends D213's far detail and D238. What is drawn of a base by distance (as authored, times the detail scale, D230): every piece as made to 25 m, the textured middle detail to 60 m, the baked far detail to 120 m (its ridge, hip and valley caps drawn twice their modelled length, `modules::FAR_REPEAT`, a roof's edge as modelled); from 120 m each section is the **hull** of its cubes (`terra_build::hull`, Unreal's approximated mesh): faces open to the air, coplanar ones of a colour merged into rectangles, cut cubes' tops at their shape's corner heights so roofs keep their pitch, each kit's materials' mean colour baked in, a slope's course one plane, 630 triangles a house where D238's proxy was 5,400; a section past 155 m (laid again within 140) holds its hull alone (`Reach::Hull`); from 600 m (handing over across ±10%, since a section and its cell are measured from different middles) each **cell** of a base, four sections a side, is one merged hull (`build::hlod`, as Unreal's HLOD merges a cell), to the horizon at 3 km, made from the cubes alone on a worker and again after a change once the camera is 300 m off. **Streaming**: a section further than 740 m is let go (its entity, meshes and pieces) and one is laid again inside 690 m; a base with nothing laid lets go of its copy and its laid pieces, so a base far off holds its cubes and its cells. Every base but the one being built is laid and drawn a **group** of sections at a time, four a side, set about its middle so a house is one (`store::GROUP`): its sections laid and drawn whole and put together, one mesh a detail and material, its meshes hung off its base, placed at the middle of its sections with anything in them and its ranges measured from there less as far as they reach and plus half a section, as an HLOD cluster switches (measured to its box, `use_aabb`, a whole house popped as it switched); the base being built keeps a section and its eight parts at a time, so an edit draws again only what it reached. Only the details in use are held: a section is laid near within 32 m (36 kept) and with its middle detail within 70 m (76 kept), each a few metres past where it is seen; every mesh's corners the same in every way welded into one (`Indexed::welded`), and every mesh but the base being built is packed (`draw::packed`, Bevy's compression: normals and tangents octahedral, texture places 16-bit, colours 8-bit, 16-bit indices where they fit; positions stay 32-bit, as Bevy 0.20's 16-bit positions drew nothing here). Each base's memory, CPU and GPU, is counted (`build.memory` over the remote; `Builds hold:` in a benchmark). Impostors (pictures from several sides) are not used: a merged hull is fewer pixels' worth of work and keeps light and shadow right; they are for shapes a hull cannot carry, as D203 found for trees. Instancing identical bases' cells is left to Bevy's batching, as bases are unique by their seeds. | Mine, on 2026-10-14, at the developers' "we're also going to need to make sure our building system is performant, both when they're in the distance (idk if they'd even be rendered?)": bases vanished past 600 m, and every section of every base stayed laid with its far and proxy meshes wherever the camera was, 860 MiB of meshes for three hundred houses seen as specks ([research/building-scale.md](../research/building-scale.md)). |
| D281 | How far an edit lays a base again | Tech | Agent | Decided | An edit lays again only the sections its pieces can change (`sections::reach`, `Whole::lay_again`): those round the sections it marked, as far as any piece reaches from the cubes it is laid from (an opening's dress, a frame's member from its post: under a section); a framed building (D236), whose members run the length of a wall, again whole; where an edit empties or first fills a column, or changes what is highest built in it, the sections that see it along the floor too, with the rows of logs along from them, as a log looks out of the building beside its wall (D262). What it does not reach is shared with the laying before, not copied. Every layout's reach is bounded as it is built in reality, so an edit's is a few metres whatever the building's size: a course of logs is jointed at places fixed along its row, a log's length apart and staggered course to course by its seed, no joint within 30 cm of a stretch's end (`layout::runs`), each log's daub, plate and sill decided by the cubes it lies along, so an edit reaches a log's reach along a row (`runs::reach_cubes`, two sections at most) and the sections that far either way are laid again; a roof's long pieces (ridges, hips, valleys, eaves, verges, battens, rafters) are jointed where their line crosses a section's edge, but within two metres of their ends (`roofs::join`), and only a roof's squares near a layout's scope are worked; a block kind's dress is laid per cube edge, its mitres from its neighbours; flights and decks are found from the layout's scope, a deck whole from its cubes there, not from every cube of the base. The base being built is drawn near and a few metres off a part of a section at a time, eight cubes a side (`sections::CLUSTER`), each part known by a number for everything its surfaces are drawn from: the pieces reaching within three cubes of it and how far in each is, the cubes from three below it up over it and three round it, the ground under them and, for a roof's, the roofs round (`sections::cluster_keys`); a section is drawn again only in the parts whose number changed, the rest left on screen as they are, and a roof's pieces are kept masked while the roofs round are as they were. Other bases are merged a section at a time, fewer meshes. The rest are kept as laid, and a section is cut in an order of its pieces themselves, so it is the same whether its base was laid whole or in part. Each kit lays again only where it can change: every kit round the marks, only log kits along the rows from them, and a roof kit only if a cube that changed is within four of a roof's (`sections::roof_near_changes`); a roof is worked out once for every kit that asks for it in one laying (`roofs::remembered`), and a layout gathers only the cubes of the kinds it looks for (`Cubes::within_where`), never every cube of the base. How far it reaches is worked out by the worker that lays the base again, which sets those sections waiting to be cut and says which lay as they were, to be left as drawn; the main thread only marks what changed, and cuts none. A base's columns are kept up to date cube by cube, not counted from every cube each operation. Tested against laying whole after edits to every kit's buildings, long halls and a walled yard (`sections::tests`). | The second goals review (research/goals-review-2026-10-14.md, finding 4): one cube in the castle re-laid all 342 of its sections, 337 ms on a worker, and the main thread then cut every one of them (55 ms). Now 198 sections are reached, laid again in 270 ms, cut on the worker; laying all of a row's sections apart looked at most of the base again for each, so they were laid together. The lead on 2026-10-14: "Castle edits take 0.3–0.5 s because log rows and dress bands span the whole castle. Bound each layout's reach the way it's built in reality." Now 24 to 54 sections are reached and laid again in 40 to 100 ms, and the castle is laid whole in 0.4 s rather than 0.95, its rows no longer each taken whole. The lead on 2026-10-14: "Castle roof re-lays should stop processing the whole connected roof." Measured as CPU time on one thread, back to back on a machine at load 35 to 50: a castle edit laid again in 60 to 115 ms rather than 120 to 205; a timber layout had walked every cube of the castle three times for its halves and posts, and each roof kit and each log kit worked out the same whole roof again. |
| D305 | Loss and repair: damage, collapse, fragments and mending | Tech | Agent | Decided | As `terra_build::loss` holds them (step 6 of [building-plan.md](building-plan.md), D193). A cube's damage is kept beside the cubes, as what it changes to is (its 32 bits are full, D231); its health is its kind's, `toughness` in its pattern's file unless its material says otherwise (D290: block 600, coursed 400, frame 300, roof 200). Harm is an operation (`Op::Damage`) with an inverse, never refused for what it brings down, as is the ground giving way (`Op::Subside`); what then hangs from nothing is found by a flood from the change on placing's rule of joined (D199), lowest first, so a hit costs what it reaches. What comes down is gone from the base, and with it the plans that hung from it alone (a ghost hangs from nothing no more than a cube does), and given back as nothing while building takes no real resources (D193 as amended); the act says it, as it was, for the game to break into fragments (`terra_build::fragments`). How a kind breaks is data, `toughness.breaks` by its pattern unless its material says: stone into chunky irregular blocks, logs into lengths and splinters, wattle, plaster and roofs into small bits, earth crumbling, each a table of pieces, sizes, throw, spin and dust. A cube destroyed bursts at once, its pieces thrown out from where it was hit, tumbling; a cube left hanging falls whole a little after what gave way under it, the higher the later, and breaks where it lands, so a collapse comes down progressively. Fragments move on a fixed step of our own, no physics engine: gravity, bounces with friction off the terrain and the base's cubes by their shapes, rolling off ledges, glancing off walls; they touch no one. They lie still, then shrink away. A puff of dust in the material's colour where they broke and where big pieces land, and the view shaken near a big collapse, through one shared effect. Drawn instanced, a bevelled box in three shapes a kind in the texture arrays (D290); capped by the preset (2,000 at high, 1,400 medium, 700 low, with fewer pieces a cube at lower presets and past 60 m), the oldest fading first past it. A damaged cube is mended before anything more goes in or comes out, so taking apart's full refund (D193) is no way round a repair, which costs its kind's repair share of what went in, a quarter of that for each quarter of health lost. Damage is drawn by darkening and flecking the colours of a cube's surfaces by the quarters it has lost, every material alike. Until attacks (D90), damage comes from the remote and a debug key. Amended by D290: near and a few metres off, a damaged cube's textured surfaces reveal a shared damage mask in the texture arrays by the health it has lost, whatever the material: hairline cracks at a quarter, wider cracks and chips at a half, flakes gone toward the end, dark in the crack, rough, the mask's normal on its walls; at the low preset, far off, and for a pack's own pieces, darkened by what is lost as before. A repaired cube draws clean. Amended 2026-10-15 (by the agent, for the developers' "look sick"): damage breaks a material by its pattern's structure, as each pattern's file says (`damage`): a block wall at its joints, then its stones' edges, then whole stones, showing its core; timber along its grain, gouged, its ends checked; a covering a shingle at a time; the stones and shingles a texture set's cells from its height map; damage spreading a little round the cubes hit. Amended again 2026-10-15 (by the agent, for the lead's "stone reads jaggy and noisy" and "logs are still faint"): damage has depth. A stone wall's surface is pushed back over each stone gone and the mortar round it, and its points beside a hole are put on the hole's outline, as a contour is marched over a grid finer than its steps on the faces a stone has gone from alone; the outline is read between texels from a distance field kept with the cells (as decals and fonts are drawn), so a hole follows its stones' edges however near, not the grid's or the texels' steps. What a point has lost is each cube round it by its loss and how near its middle the point is, smooth from cube to cube, and more in a wall's weaker patches, so a battered wall gives way in clumps and a stone gives way whole by what was lost at its middle, the same on the CPU and the GPU. A log or beam harmed past half loses chunks out of its outline, so deep as its pattern says (`Grain` `chunks`), the fresh wood bared, and its sawn ends splinter back; its splits widen to a hand's width with torn pale lips. A covering's shingles gone show its battens and rafters. Amended again the same day (by the agent, for the lead's "logs read as melted wax" and "keep stones rigid"): each stone is judged once, by what was lost at its middle, which every texel of it finds the same (the cells keep the way to it in whole texels), and the surface marks its sunk points (their wear), which the shader draws as the hollow, so a stone is whole and flat to its face or gone, the same on screen as in the geometry; a log's chunks are cut by flat faces, an axe's V across the grain or an impact's one face slanting in along it, shaded flat with hard edges, the wood under them bared and pale, the rest of the log keeping its round; its splits are straight cracks down the grain; its sawn ends splinter into long thin spikes along it. Fragments stay cubes, as the developers asked ("explode into voxels"): a timber one is a piece of its log, its ends across the grain the sawn end with its rings, its sides bark, where it was split fresh wood, turned along the wall it fell from; a block of cubes bursts into its cubes and their pieces where it first lands; a piece on a slope steeper than about 20 degrees, a roof's, slides down it and off its eave. A stone's hole is deeper than a stone is thick (16 cm), its floor the wall's core in shadow, near black at its back, its sides plain mortar; a big fallen piece lies 8 to 10 s before it fades, a small one 2 to 3, within the fragments' cap. A texture set not yet resident is drawn plain, never in another slot's pictures, which had shown the damage mask's as black cracks on undamaged daub. | Mine, building step 6 on 2026-10-14, the developers having decided collapse into debris (D193) and said of the remaining work "Like get it done". Valheim's and Rust's structures take damage, collapse when unsupported and are repaired by the hammer for materials; 7 Days to Die's collapse drops rubble. Property tests over random operations hold it: nothing floats after any hit or subsidence, what is in, came out and was lost is what went in, and undo is exact (`ops/tests.rs`, `loss/tests.rs`, `tests/rules.rs`); live, a log wall's first course knocked out brings the wall down in fragments that are gone eight seconds on (`tools/sandbox/rules.ts`). Debris piles to gather were built first and replaced the same day, at the developers' word (D193 as amended). A castle tower's top cut off (`build.damage` with `build: "castle"`, Windows GPU): 2,208 cubes fall into 256 piles, worked out in 10 to 13 ms on the main thread; the piles made on a worker and on screen in 120 to 200 ms; the sections in 220 ms with frames at their usual pace, 0.65 to 1.2 s while another run loaded the machine. Damage drawn as vertex colour reads as a dark scorch on a block surface, whose triangles are few where it is flat (D284); cracks would need the material's shader. |
| D355 | Destruction leaves a blueprint to rebuild | Design | Developers | Decided | Later, not in the current baseline (D333): what is destroyed or collapses without the player taking it apart with the build tools (an attack, the ground giving way) is not lost; each lost cube is left as a blueprint in its place, so it can be built again. Taking apart with the hammer stays as D193 has it, leaving nothing. Until this is built, collapse and damage work as D305 says, including plans hanging from what falls going with it. | The developers on 2026-10-15: "future design note, not part of this scope: destruction shouldn't be permanent. that is, we'll want destruction to leave like a blueprint in its place when its not done intentionally by the player with the build tools (so it can be rebuilt)". |
| D343 | Nothing but a roof on a roof | Design | Developers | Decided | A cube that is not a roof's is never placed resting on a roof's cube or in a roof cube's place, and a roof's cube is never put in under one that is not a roof's: refused, "Take the roof off to build above it." Roof cubes on roof cubes stay allowed: dormers, valleys and roofs that merge. To add a storey, the roof is taken off (taken apart and given back as any part is), the storey built, and a roof added again over it. Every way cubes are placed is held to it, as it is a check in `terra_build::ops` (`Base::on_roof`) on laying a plan, putting a cube in and changing a material: the hammer, brushes, the room and wall tools, change material, the remote, undo and redo; the village's houses are laid by the same checks, a wall dormer's face standing on its front wall and the house with a timber storey floored over in stone. Amends D226 (any number of storeys, built over the walls, not over a roof) and D241 (roof cubes laid by hand on anything built, other than one under a wall). | The developers on 2026-10-15, watching the build film: "youve also got some weird stuff in here where you try to add the second story with the roof still built and theres some issues (you wouldnt really wanna do that and we might wanna just not allow you to build non-roof voxels on the roof voxels?)". Tested in `tests/rules.rs` (`nothing_but_a_roof_is_built_on_a_roof`) and by every village building, the town and the castle put up by the operations. |
| D348 | The voxel contract: who draws what between cubes, and every junction proved | Tech | Agent | Decided | [building-voxels.md](building-voxels.md) is the contract for construction cubes: the cube and its 32 shapes, who owns every face, edge, corner and piece between two cubes (each exactly one cube, line or prop), ten invariants that keep a building closed (the cubes the truth, one owner, every shown face and junction closed, an eighth of a cube, sections agree, every detail and every state closes, a material adds no junction, what cannot close is refused, every junction proved), and the junction matrix: each pair of neighbours by pattern, shape and state, with the rule that closes it and the test or inspected case that proves it; a row without one is a gap, listed there with its recommended rule. Where roofs and walls meet is building-plan.md's design (its R1 to R7), cited by reference. A change to how cubes are laid or drawn keeps the invariants or changes the page in the same commit; a new pattern adds its rows. | The developers on 2026-10-15: "i think its worthwhile to have a very clear design doc on how the voxels need to work so we can make sure there's no gaps?", after "we should implement it correct, but the issue is we had gaps, because we didnt think this through". Mine: the rules were spread over construction-001, building-kits.md, building-drawing.md and the code, and no one place said which junctions had a rule and a check. Probing each junction with the mesh checker for the page found nine gaps, among them walls through roofs, roofs falling to walls, cubes meeting only along an edge, roof kinds cut to stairs, holes in roofs and the states the checker never sees. Two invariants untested until now gained tests: every edge with a role dressed by one cube (`roles`), and every open side of a roof's covering closed by one edge piece (`roofs`). |
| D306 | Decks framed on their posts, and stairs that bear | Tech | Agent | Decided | Amends D206 and D220. A timber deck is framed within its layer of cubes as a carpenter frames one (`layout::floors`): boards on top; joists across them on every line between cubes, the outermost its edges, where it is in the air; a rim joist as deep as they are across their ends on every open side, a ledger where they run into a wall, and on the ground a rim the layer's whole depth; and at the layer's foot a bearer, the kit's heavy timber as it dresses `Role::Crest`, along every line of posts under the deck, the fewer of the two ways (else the joists square to the walls the deck meets, else the boards along its longer side). Every post under a deck ends at the bearer's underside, so a deck on posts in its corner cubes overhangs its bearers by the rest of the cube. A post's top cube with nothing on it beside a deck on its layer, as the hammer's post a storey high leaves under a floor laid at its top, is the deck's: boarded over, the post or stone pillar ending under it (`floors::post_in_deck`). A layout sets what rests on a member against its real size from the kit's data (`timber::section`), never an offset kept for a kit, as the model contract (D303) asks of every piece. A flight's stringers are seated on the floor at their foot and run up until their lower corner meets the back of the top step, where they bear on a deck's rim, which the deck lays where a flight meets it; posts stand under a stringer only to keep its span within 1.8 m, evenly along it, and under its head where nothing stands behind its top step. The hammer lays a footing under a post or a flight wherever its foot rests on the ground, on a hillside a storey up too, not only on the ground storey; a post's footing has its top at the ground, filling the cube the ground passes through, so the post is never buried (`ground::post_on_ground`); and a flight climbs onto what is ahead of it, a deck two storeys over its foot as well as one (`ground::flight_onto`). Checked without a picture by `terra_build/tests/decks.rs`; the kit sheet's `decks` rows show each case in logs, hewn timber and on stone piers. | The developers on 2026-10-14, of the cabin's deck: "the poles or w/e shouldnt be disconnected on the sides, they should be under the flat part", and "its important that these kits are cleanly designed so each one doesnt have to be customized by the game". The hammer's posts stood up through the deck's corner cubes; the bolster under a deck was set by a log's diameter where a hewn member is a course deep, so it hung 7 cm under the deck; the rim was a 46 cm stack of boxes; stringers stopped short of the deck and their posts stood a spacing apart regardless of span. Real decks and Valheim's and Enshrouded's stand posts under bearers, joists on those and boards on the joists. |
| D320 | What every kit's surfaces are shaded and mapped by, beyond its own data | Tech | Agent | Decided | Three rules in shared code, so no kit needs its own (the developers: kits "cleanly designed so each one doesnt have to be customized by the game"): a face looking down is shaded as a modelled piece's baked occlusion would shade it, up to two thirds of its light within about 55 degrees of straight down (`dress::underside`, every surface a kit draws), because the sky light lights an underside by the sunlit ground's whole bounce; each sawn end takes its member's shade and warmth and then its own, a tenth lighter or darker and greyed toward its lightness by up to six tenths (`timber::end_colour`), so a wall's ends read as cut at different times and weathered differently rather than one sticker; and a board drawn from one plank of its texture (`strips`) takes the plank across every face of it, running on from face to face round its section, not one column smeared down its sides; and a long wall's joints shifted from course to course by the golden ratio of a log's length, so no two courses within four of each other are jointed together (D178's stagger had lined up every other course), none in a cube another wall crosses, and the daub jointed a few centimetres off the logs' joint; a modelled verge board seated square to its roof, unsheared, its ends cut plumb (`Line::plumb`); a ridge capping turned over as much of its width as its thickness swings through at the ridge (`roofing::crest_blend`); and relief mapping faded out as the view grazes a surface or where it turns sharply, over tangents weighted by area as MikkTSpace weighs them; a flight of ramps' copings run on from stone to stone, mitred to a level coping at a ramp's head and foot, run down over a half beside its foot, its square ends cut plumb; hips' cappings turned as the ridge's but near their tops; and a room's light probe its own light from every way and its own reflection, a fifth of it, not the open sky's (D155); and what faces up under a roof shaded as its sky is hidden (`dress::sheltered`). `cargo xtask texels` measures how far each kit's textures are stretched, from the meshes. | The kit review ([research/kit-review-2026-10-14.md](../research/kit-review-2026-10-14.md)): lintels' soffits and every quoin's underside lit brighter and greener than the wall's shaded face; every log end and hewn end one tint, bright beside weathered faces; joists' and rim beams' sides one column of their plank, 99:1. The developers: "theres still imperfections in some of the kits". |
| D339 | Acts on a base worked out off the main thread and swapped in | Tech | Agent | Decided | An act on the player's base (D21's operations: a fill, a carve, damage and what it brings down) may be worked out off the main thread (`build::act::Building::act_later`): against a copy of the base, which shares its cubes until they change (D213), the operations are applied and what falls, is chipped off and is only repainted worked out (`build::work_out`) on a thread of the acts' own, so an act of a few milliseconds never waits behind the section workers' longer jobs; the main thread swaps the result in whole (`Build::swap`), the old base let go on a worker, pays for it, keeps it to be undone and logs it, as a streaming engine double-buffers what its workers build. Acts are taken in in the order made, and anything that reads or changes the base for an act (another act, a check of one, an undo, a remote call) first takes in every act still out, so the outcome is the same as acting at once whatever the timing. Harm's damage, the remote's calls in a benchmark (which nothing waits on) and the hammer's steps are worked out so, a step checked on the main thread first so a refusal is told at once, and the next step chosen once it is in, its moment shortened by the wait. A scripted run that is not a benchmark acts at once, so the QA films are the same frame for frame. A change's time to the screen is counted from its act. | The castle QA's tower fall spent 15 to 100 ms of the main thread on damage and collapse in the frame it was made, and a storey's fills up to 67 ms. The lead: "apply operations, including damage and collapse, asynchronously behind a final swap". With D340, the stone build film's hitches went from 49 to none or few and the tower's worst frame from 45 to under 26.7 ms (RTX 5090). |
| D282 | A calmer wind: gusts to one and a half times the steady wind, leaf clusters that rustle as one | Tech | Agent | Decided | Amends D255. Gusts run from under a half to one and a half times the old steady wind, about the same on average, not a fifth to two and a half, at which a gust swung an oak's crown a metre. A leaf card's flutter takes its phase from where it is at a branch's scale (a third of a radian a metre, not two), so a card's corners move together, and shakes 1.5 cm, not 5: a card is a cluster of leaves. Trees, the world's flora and grass share the gusts; grass keeps its own flutter. | The developers on 2026-10-14: "very bubble trees, like the shake effect was a bit crazy". Measured on a still view of crowns at 15:00: the picture changed 2.2% between frames a tenth of a second apart (0.07% with the trees' wind off), three quarters of it from the cards' flutter; now 1.2%. |
| D266 | The haze under trees | Tech | Agent | Decided | The volumetric haze (D217, D244) takes its sky light at 0.015, a third of before, and the green-grey of light come through leaves rather than the open sky's blue; its own colour is a warm off-white. Under a canopy the sun lights it only in shafts, so that blue fill was most of what the haze showed, a grey-blue veil over everything a few tens of metres off, worst on the dark spruces. | Found by the trees' work (D264): the veil over the spruces went away with the haze off. The shafts at 17:00 hold. |
| D351 | Building's code a module a concern, and what nothing uses deleted | Tech | Agent | Decided | `terra_build` keeps one concern a module: the snapshot of now in `save` (older formats refused, D353), the quick tools' shortcuts and wall tiles in `shortcuts`, the hammer's ray in `ray`, the base and its cubes in `lib.rs`. Code, data fields, options and remote parameters nothing uses are deleted, not kept (AGENTS.md); one weld and one tangent path, `mesh.rs`'s `Indexed`, which the game draws with. What is left, ranked, with its order: [research/building-code-health-2026-10-15.md](../research/building-code-health-2026-10-15.md). | The developers on 2026-10-15: "make sure we've not overloaded a bunch of baggage too. weve had lots of changes in the system so we want to keep the code well organized". |
| D353 | No old saves | Tech | Developers | Decided | Only the save format of now is read: a game save (`play::save`, version 2) or a base's snapshot (`terra_build::save`, version 2) of any other version is refused before anything of it loads, said as "saved by an older build; start a new game", never converted. The readers of older formats are gone: shortcut lists, walls on edges and rooms (D152), D209's roof sections, glass (D225), D231's four-bit shapes, items saved by model file. Amends D160, D201, D231 and D241. | The developers on 2026-10-15: "we dont care about old saves"; "we dont even care about saves right now". |

## Art, characters and sound

The look, models, bodies and gear, textures and audio.

| # | Topic | Owner | By | Status | Decision | Why, or what closes it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D14 | Art direction | Design | Developers | Decided | Textured, lightly stylised realism: real materials in detailed textures, a little shaded and simplified, neither photoreal nor cartoon, with strong silhouettes and true proportions at life size (D110). The main guideline for everything the game draws, one style guide for every realm and setting: [art-style.md](art-style.md). Models carry their textures (colour, normal and roughness maps, as Tripo makes them); a realm brings its own materials rather than a palette. | The developers on 2026-10-12, after seeing a log cabin with flat colour and with Tripo's textures kept ([research/realistic-building-textures.md](../research/realistic-building-textures.md)): "We can use realistic textures but they should be a little shaded. We're not aiming for photo realistic, but not cartoon-y." Earlier the same day: direction D, graphic layers, flat colour by palette role, chosen from the mood boards to be tried; the developers found it "a little too cartoony" and "low poly". |
| D291 | Leaves true to life, not flat | Design | Developers | Decided | Amends D14 for foliage. A forest's leaves are drawn as real leaves seen in a real wood, as Sons of the Forest's are: each its own shape, veins, relief and tilt, catching the light and glinting one by one, in the varied greens of real leaves, and lit through from behind; not flat fills of one tone per leaf, which the rest of the guide's "a little shaded, a little simplified" had let them become. The rest of the world keeps D14. | The developers on 2026-10-14: "i think the tree leaves still have this flat color style that we need to fix. these come from the old design system we wanted, but let's make it much better, not this flat style." |
| D54 | Models, for now | Tech | Agent | Leaning | Free CC0 low-polygon packs (Quaternius), read for their shapes only and given our own colours in code, so every model shares one palette and one shader. A prop with no free model comes from [tools/props](../tools/props/README.md) (D147). | Made the outpost scene possible in a day with no artist, and keeps the door open to replacing any model without touching the look. Untested: whether stock shapes can carry the game's own identity, and what a dead world is built from. Sources and licences are in [THIRD_PARTY.md](../THIRD_PARTY.md). |
| D112 | How body templates are made | Tech | Agent | Leaning | One base body, made once and checked: one mesh (our own base male, made by Tripo) in our T-pose, our skeleton, skin weights made for it, skin patches and socket rules. A body is a few parameters on it, as modern games do: proportions as bone scales, build as a few soft morphs, the head as blends of head shapes, and a painted, tinted skin. The creator's sliders (D111) are those numbers, clamped; a template is a preset of them; an NPC is a template with offsets from its seed. A template from a design gets estimated parameters and a skin Tripo paints on the base's layout. The morphs themselves are made once each from designs of the base person (heavier, thinner, stronger, a woman), the base's mesh registered onto each. Hair is a separate part bound to the head like gear. See [characters.md](characters.md). | Separate models each need their own rig, and gear made and checked against each, so gear costs items times bodies. Shipped games keep one skeleton per family and put shape mostly in the bones, with a few morphs copied onto every garment (Red Dead Redemption 2, Starfield, Final Fantasy XIV, Bannerlord; [modern-character-variety.md](../research/modern-character-variety.md)). Fitting a shared mesh freely to each generated body copied its creases, broke faces and failed the rig checks (2026-10-12). Untested: whether a few parameters reach our designs, which the spike answers. |
| D113 | How sound is put together | Tech | Agent | Decided | Gameplay says what happened as a `Noise` (what, where), or it is heard from state it already shows; one module decides what each noise sounds like. Sounds are entities with a `Voice` (part of the mix, level), so the options menu's volumes reach what is already playing. Walkers carry `Footsteps`, sounding things carry `Ambient` and play only within earshot. Clips come from named sets with several takes; air, water, fire, birds and music are generated in code. Bevy's own audio, no new dependency. Scripted runs are silent but report what they would have played; WSL is silent unless `--sound`. | Asked for by the developers on 2026-10-11, done systemically and in ECS. A noise as a fact rather than a file keeps gameplay free of sound, and lets creatures hear it later. Bevy's audio has no reverb or muffling; `bevy_seedling` is the candidate if we need them. See [audio.md](audio.md). |
| D114 | What the game sounds like, for now | Design | Agent | Leaning | Sound carries information; music is sparse and follows the place: phrases with long silences, warmer near a settlement, low and uneasy near danger, made in code. Ambience follows where you stand: wind, birds where it is safe, water near the shore, fires, the hum where magic pools, a settlement's bell. Clips are CC0 packs as a start. | From [player.md](player.md) ("sound carries information... music sparse, and from the world where it can be"), built so it can be heard. Questions for the developers in [audio.md](audio.md#questions-for-the-developers). |
| D115 | How characters are made and rigged | Tech | Agent | Leaning | A character's base body is drawn as a design sheet in a T-pose, made into a textured model on Tripo, and rigged with Tripo's current (V3) auto-rig in its Unreal skeleton, both on the Studio subscription. Our skeleton is the one Quaternius's animation libraries use (Universal Animation Library 1 and 2, bought, current versions), which has Unreal's bone names too; the clips the game plays are cut from them into `models/character/mannequin.glb`. A script moves our joints to Tripo's, keeps our bones' directions, keeps Tripo's weights by name, and adds Tripo's four twist bones a side. The clips put a limb's whole roll in the forearm and shin; as each clip is sampled for a body, that roll moves to the hand and foot and the twist bone takes half (Unreal's convention), and bodies and gear are skinned weight-blended: dual quaternions but round the hips (`models/character/skinning.json`). A clip check passes every clip first; a rig checker passes the body: same skeleton and rest pose as ours, at most four bones a vertex, nothing driven by a distant bone, left and right alike, seams that stay closed, and through our animations no worse than 3.5 times the mannequin. Each character is exported with our animations re-sampled on its own skeleton, and looked at in the sandbox's workbench before use. | The developers asked for a pipeline that lets non-artists make good characters with Tripo doing the hard parts. Tried on 2026-10-11: generating in an A-pose and moving the body into our T-pose crushed the shoulders in every animation; Blender's own weights and the mannequin's copied weights both did worse than Tripo's converted rig, which matched the mannequin in everyday moves. Still to do: strip the clips' unchanging joint translations, since Bevy applies them and would undo our moved joints. See [research/ai-models-for-art.md](../research/ai-models-for-art.md#characters-made-and-rigged) and [tools/characters](../tools/characters/README.md). |
| D120 | Scripting Tripo | Tech | Agent | Decided | One command-line tool, [tools/tripo](../tools/tripo), does everything the Studio web app does to a model (images, views, models, texture, retopology, segmenting, UVs, rigging, animation, export, import) on the Studio subscription's credits, through the app's private backend. TypeScript on Node with no dependencies (D121); every command prints the project it worked on so the next can take it. The bake-off's API client is retired. | The subscription's credits are what we pay for, and Studio is where Tripo's newest models and tools are; one tool means one place to fix when Tripo changes its requests, and anyone, person or agent, can run it with nothing to install. Every request was captured from the app or read from its code, and every command was run ([research/tripo-studio-api.md](../research/tripo-studio-api.md)). |
| D128 | How equipment fits every body | Tech | Agent | Leaning | Every item fits every body by contract, not by fitting per character: one base body and skeleton (D112); clothing and armour made once on it, as the faces it covers pushed out or as pieces bound to its surface, loose parts to a helper; each follows any body by the shared skeleton's proportions and carries the base's build morphs, copied by a tool, rigid parts riding one bone; one mesh per item shared by every wearer; each says which body patches it covers, which are then not drawn; a template where an item fails gets that item's own model by exception. Rigid worn things made once in a socket's space; held things once to the hand socket's axes. Checks run on every item on every template and at each parameter's limits. See [characters.md](characters.md). | Researched on 2026-10-11 and 2026-10-12 ([character-creators.md](../research/character-creators.md), [one-mesh-for-every-body.md](../research/one-mesh-for-every-body.md), [modern-character-variety.md](../research/modern-character-variety.md)) after gear fitted object by object clipped and sat wrong. The developers answered on 2026-10-12: the standard slots (D64) and no layers for now. Making each piece on each base body worked but cost a generated model per item per body; the developers asked for a way that is not items times bodies, and from how modern games do it. |
| D147 | Props with no free model | Tech | Agent | Leaning | A prop no free pack covers is made by [tools/props](../tools/props/README.md): a concept drawn to the style guide ([art-style.md](art-style.md)), a low-poly Tripo model of it (P1, textured at 2K with its normal, roughness and metal maps, about 50 Studio credits), then a free clean-up in Blender that keeps its textures (D304) and makes it to the model contract (D303); a tool and the modelled trees are still given the nearest of the palette roles their `prop.json` lists, and coloured by role in the game (D54). Its concept, model and `prop.json` are source art in `art/props/`; the game's file is remade from them for free. Nothing is shaped from primitives in code any more. Every catalogue item states how wide, high and deep a real one is, and the model contract's checker holds its model to that (D110, D303); the sandbox's workbench lines props up beside a body as the game draws them (`--prop`). | The bake-off's route (research/ai-models-for-art.md), kept as a tool since we keep needing props (D71). Generated models match their concepts far better than modelling by code, and the clean-up keeps them in one palette and one shader. The clean-up still flat-shades every face; the style guide wants normals split only at creases (D14). |
| D186 | What the base body wears | Design | Developers | Decided | The base bodies wear plain, close-fitting underwear (briefs; on the female, briefs and a band top), painted on with almost no relief, so no one is ever naked and nothing stands off the skin for gear to clear or for the skin tint to tell from skin. What a character starts in, such as today's shorts and undershirt, is ordinary worn items (D64, D128) that can be taken off or replaced. | The developers on 2026-10-12, after the shorts and tops sculpted into the base bodies were found in the way of tight gear and the skin tint: "yeah thats probably easier, lets do that". Survival games (Rust, ARK, Valheim, Conan Exiles), Skyrim and WoW do the same. The base bodies are redrawn in it, then their rigs, morphs and gear rebuilt; see [characters.md](characters.md). |
| D188 | Texture budgets for characters and gear | Tech | Agent | Decided | Source art keeps Tripo's maps as made (4096: colour, normal, roughness and metal). The game's files take each kind of asset at its budget: a body's maps at 2048, a worn item's at 1024, scaled when the build writes them (`--textures=` in `build_bodies.sh`). Garments cut from the body bake the dressed model's normals and roughness as well as its colour. Compressed, mipmapped textures (KTX2) come next. | Realistic characters (D186) get their look from the normal and roughness maps, not the triangles ([research/realistic-building-textures.md](../research/realistic-building-textures.md)), so the maps are kept. At 4096 one body's three maps take about 250 MB of GPU memory uncompressed, and the body file grew from 7 to 13 MB. Shipped games keep source art large and set budgets per kind of asset. |
| D303 | The model contract | Tech | Agent | Decided | Every model the game places obeys one checked contract ([art-style.md](art-style.md#the-model-contract)): its catalogue entry states its real size (`dims`, metres) and its file is that size within 2% or a centimetre; glTF's axes, +Y up and its front +Z; its origin a defined anchor by kind (a floor prop's base middle, a leaf's hinge foot, a wall-hung prop's back, a frame's opening bottom middle, a kit piece's grid place, D208); named sockets as `socket.<name>` empties (a leaf's `hinge` and `handle`, a seat's `seat`) that code uses; and a door's or shutters' leaf fills the first hole it fits. `tools/props` makes models so from `prop.json`; `cargo xtask contract` checks them, in `cargo xtask check`; the workbench draws each one's box, origin and sockets. No per-model offset, turn or scale in code or catalogue: the catalogue's `size`, `height`, `lift`, `seat` and a fitting's `hinge` side are gone, refused by the catalogue's reader. Amended 2026-10-15 (by the agent, for the fourth goals review: "'no scale in code' contradicts the stretched doors and frame members"): fitting by sockets is the contract's one stretch. A fitted model is stretched only along the axes its kind fits along, by what the layout fits it to, never by a number of its own: a leaf across and up its hole from its hinge, a kit's module along its line from its `start` socket to its `end` in whole copies; generated members (logs, frame members) are made to their line, not stretched models. Every kit piece states its size and the kit checker holds it: a module its `dims` and sockets, and now a corner stone (quoins, jamb stones) its `dims`, its dressed faces' box from the wall's corner at its origin. | The developers on 2026-10-14: "one requirement of all these is dimensions/center points/etc because otherwise props like doors or windows will never render correctly (or position correctly)", and "thats why its important that these kits are cleanly designed so each one doesnt have to be customized by the game". Shipped pipelines hold meshes to a pivot and socket convention (Unreal's pivots and sockets) for the same reason. |
| D304 | Props drawn in their own textures | Tech | Agent | Decided | A prop keeps the textures Tripo made, by default: `props.ts model` adds Tripo's normal, roughness and metal maps to a P1 model (`pbr`, 5 credits), and `refine` keeps colour and normal maps at 2K for a prop 1.5 m or more and 1K below, roughness and metal at half, stored as JPEG, tangents exported. The game draws each textured part in its own material, textures loaded by label from the file through Bevy's glTF loader (mipmapped, D14); a part named `Fire` glows in its own texture; a flat part of an older model is still coloured by role. A tint by role or material goes in the material's base colour, never by flattening. `palette: true` keeps a tool or tree in roles. GPU-compressed textures (KTX2, BC7) wait for the asset pipeline step D188 names. | The prop renderer read only points and normals, so every prop had been flattened to role colours, against D14 and the demos' look; the chest, the well and the stall stood flat beside textured houses. The textures were in the source models all along. |
| D189 | Modular packs and belts | Tech | Agent | Decided | A pack is a bag and a harness. The bag is any generated model, rigid on the back socket, its own straps cut away, fitted so its corners meet the harness's strap ends. The harness, the straps, is made once per kind on the base body as a garment is (D128), its path and look taken from a model of the base person wearing one, so it bends with the shoulders and fits every body. A pack in the catalogue names its bag and its harness. Things hung from a belt hang from its standard points, by where each hangs (a quiver from its mouth), and swing loose about it as spring bones do. See [characters.md](characters.md#rigid-worn-things-helmets-packs-quivers-belt-pouches). | The developers on 2026-10-12: "gear has to be modular", asking what happens with a different backpack. Straps cut from a generated pack are stiff loops in one pose, fitted to no body; straps made per pack would cost work per item (D127, D182). Games keep a few harnesses and many bags, attached at points. Straps coded as routes and painted one colour looked wrong beside realistic textures (D186). The swing is the first part of the spring bones planned for coat tails and hair. |
| D190 | How the ground and the light are drawn | Tech | Agent | Decided | Covered ground is drawn from real textures, soil, grass, rock and sand in one texture array, projected along all three axes (triplanar) and blended by what grows there, each tinted by the planet's colour for it so a planet still sets its ground's colours. The shade is lit by the sky above and the sunlit ground below, from a small cube map painted from the sky's colours and filtered by Bevy each frame (`GeneratedEnvironmentMapLight`), not one flat ambient colour. The air, the exposure and tone mapping are D212. | The developers said the scene had gaps toward realism, "might be lighting and ground textures" (2026-10-12). Triplanar texturing and sky light are what shipped games with voxel ground do; a painted cube map follows our own sky and its day, where a captured one would not. |
| D191 | Where materials come from | Tech | Agent | Decided | Tileable materials (ground, plaster, planks, stone) come first from free CC0 libraries, Poly Haven foremost, fetched by `tools/textures`; what no library has, such as a roof of shakes, is generated with Tripo and made to tile by the same tool; models, such as a kit's stone, come from `tools/props`. Bought packs only where their licence allows use in a game shared as a public repository. Every material's source is in `assets/materials/README.md`. | The developers asked whether bought packs would be needed and said they were acceptable "as long as it wont prevent us from achieving the very large set we'll ultimately need" (2026-10-12). CC0 needs no licence tracking and no money, and covers the common surfaces. |
| D202 | How ground cover is drawn | Tech | Agent | Decided | The cover tier of [biomes.md](biomes.md#three-tiers-by-who-has-to-agree) is `terra_world::cover`, fed by a `CoverRules` the world fills in: a field of how much grass grows at each place, how lush, where paths are, and how much leaf litter and moss. Grass is blades generated round the camera in 8 m squares on other threads, after Ghost of Tsushima: each blade placed alone on a jittered grid (about 280 a square metre at most, a third of them short, and half-thick grass keeping most of its blades), belonging to the nearest of a scatter of clumps that share height, facing and colour, each a Bézier curve with rounded normals; three rings of detail, the far ones a subset of the near one's blades chosen by rank, a square changing ring by its nearest point with hysteresis, and the shader thinning blades one by one by rank before a ring change so none pops (the far-lasting blades are wider in every ring, so none changes width); the vertex shader shrinks blades into their roots from 30 m, so the grass has no edge. The ground under it is painted by the same field, as Horizon Zero Dawn's placement paints its terrain: a 128 m map round the camera that the ground shader reads, turning grass to leaf litter under trees (Poly Haven's Forest Leaves 02, a fifth layer of the ground's stack), adding moss, and darkening the ground under thick grass as the blades' own shade would. The bare-ground measure of biomes.md (the share of ground within 30 m that nothing covers, and of that, paths) is printed with every `--shots` picture and on the vantage sheet. | The developers on 2026-10-13: "grass is pretty bad right now. We should take care of it and make sure that grass can be nice and not spotty", and the forest's other foliage should look like the realistic world we are moving to. The spike's tufts on a 0.35 m grid left most of the ground showing between them. On the RTX 3080 the walk bench is unchanged (6.2 ms median); the lush forest's floor went from 22% bare to 9%, nearly all of it paths. |
| D203 | How trees change detail | Tech | Agent | Decided | A tree's levels of detail switch at distances that grow with its height (30, 75, 180 and 420 m for a 20 m tree, clamped to half and 1.4 times that), so detail follows its size on screen, as Unreal's screen-size LODs do; each change dithers over a fifth of its distance (Bevy's `VisibilityRange`, measured from the camera in the shadow pass too), so no hysteresis is needed. A crown's shadow stand-ins (cards, then solid lumps) switch at the tree's own levels, never between them. Past the third level a tree is its crown's solid lumps in the leaves' colour over its simplest trunk, out to 420 m, so the forest carries to the horizon. A bush is drawn once, its shadow from a copy only the sun sees. Real impostors (pictures of each tree from several sides) only if the far lumps prove wrong up close to their distance. Checked with `cargo xtask pops`, which finds what pops or flickers between consecutive frames. | The developers on 2026-10-13: "things flicker in and out, the shades are working weird". Detail switched at fixed distances whatever a tree's size, crossfades were a flat 6 m, crown shadows switched at 25 and 80 m while the trees switched at 30 and 75, a bush dithered between two copies of itself over 2 m, and every tree vanished at 180 m. The walk bench on the RTX 3080 went from 6.2 to 5.8 ms. |
| D204 | How sun shadows are kept steady and let through | Tech | Agent | Decided | The sun's shadow direction moves in steps of a twentieth of a degree rather than every frame, since a light turning by a sliver each frame redraws every shadow edge differently and they crawl; Bevy's cascades already keep their size and snap to whole texels, so the camera moving does not. Contact shadows (Bevy's, marched 0.4 m through the depth buffer) on the camera and the sun, where feet, roots and blades meet the ground. A crown's solid shadow lumps are 0.6 of its size, so a low sun looking through many crowns still lands in shafts and patches. | The developers on 2026-10-13: "the shades are working weird"; at 17:00 the lumps put the whole lush forest floor in shade. Contact shadows cost nothing measurable on the RTX 3080 (walk 5.8 ms). |
| D214 | Temporal anti-aliasing, eye adaptation and bark that reads | Tech | Agent | Decided | The camera resolves with Bevy's temporal anti-aliasing instead of SMAA, as every game with dense foliage does, with `ShadowFilteringMethod::Temporal`; every wind shader gives last frame's position from last frame's time (`globals.time - globals.delta_time`), so swaying leaves and blades do not smear. Foliage draws its crossfades between levels of detail with its own fragment shaders, adapted from Bevy's (MIT or Apache-2.0), dithering in Jimenez's interleaved gradient noise shifted every frame, as Unreal dithers its foliage, since Bevy's fixed 4×4 pattern cannot be resolved by TAA and shows as a crawling screen door. The eye adapts (Bevy's `AutoExposure`) round D212's exposure for the hour, at most two stops brighter in deep shade and one darker in glare, by a compensation curve flat over that band. Bark is a scanned furrowed bark (Poly Haven's Bark Willow 02, CC0) with its normal, occlusion and roughness maps, at sRGB 112 or so, the middle of real bark's range; the trunks' triangles were wound inward, so the inside of the far wall was drawn, lit from behind, and every trunk was black. | The developers on 2026-10-13, after a video of the walk: "The bark is very dark, the things pop in and out, it doesn't yet feel like AAA game". What shipped games do and what Bevy 0.20 has for it is [research/aaa-forest-look.md](../research/aaa-forest-look.md). The walk bench on the RTX 3080 went from 6.2 to 7.1 ms, within budget; recorded. |
| D216 | Light under the canopy, and leaves that give way to the camera | Tech | Agent | Decided | The sky lights shade 1.4 times as brightly as D212's keys, since open sky gives level ground about a sixth of the sun's light and they gave it a sixteenth, so every shadow was near black; the grading adds no contrast in the shadows. Under a canopy the sky light is dimmed and warmed toward what leaves and ground bounce, as Crysis bakes into its trees' vertices: a trunk's vertices carry how much sky they see (little low on the trunk, most in the crown), and everything under the canopy takes its sky light from the sky occlusion volume (D270). Foliage within 1.6 m of the camera fades out in a moving dither for TAA to blend, so the camera does not push through solid leaves; shadows keep it. The eye adapts up to one and a quarter stops brighter (D286), round a metered brightness 1.1 stops below D214's, with no extra saturation: a slightly darker, more muted scene, as the Witcher 3's. | The developers on 2026-10-13: "lets try those" (warmer light under the canopy, leaf shading, fog and sun shafts, leaves fading at the camera, more kinds of plant), after [research/aaa-forest-look.md](../research/aaa-forest-look.md). In shade the trunks read as blue-black and the litter as black. |
