The oak split into all its limbs at one point, its crown a ball of leaves to the ground, each card two to four metres across carrying a dozen leaves the size of plates, clumps only at twig ends. 23 models in all, told apart by kind, form and height, each drawn turned, scaled and tinted where it stands. Measured on the RTX 3080 against the commit before: the walk bench 11.0 ms median (99th percentile 12.1) against 11.2 (12.9); `lodcheck` worst frame 0.05% on the forest floor against 0.33%, 0.01% on the path as before. |
| D222 | How trees, bushes, small plants and rocks are made | Tech | Agent | Decided | Trees, bushes, herbs and flowers are grown by rules, as SpeedTree makes them for shipped games (`terra_world::grown`): a kind in `flora.ron` gives its habit (spreading as an oak, conical as a pine, upright as a birch) or its shape, its bark (a material) and its leaves (a card), and a few numbers; each kind is grown as a few variants from the world's seed (six pines, four birches, six broadleaves, four bushes, three berry bushes, four of each herb, six flowers), each drawn at D203's and D221's levels of detail, one set of cards thinning, with its shadow stand-ins, its far crown as lumps whose normals lean out of the whole crown. Leaves, fronds and flowers are cards in one atlas, each cut to an octagon round what it covers (`textures.ts cards`, from Tripo pictures on a blue background, keyed out), with mipmaps that keep their coverage (`mipmaps`, any picture with transparency). Bark is a material (Poly Haven's pine and brown bark, a generated birch bark). Trees are grown at the mighty end of their kind's real heights (docs/art-style.md): pines 16 to 27 m, birches 13 to 20 m, broadleaves 11 to 19 m, their sprays larger to stay within the near tree's budget, wood a fifth of an item a metre, and great trees, which only iron fells (D96), from 22 m. Rocks are shapes from Poly Haven's CC0 scans, a piece of a set where it is one, cut to 350 to 550 triangles (`tools/props/scans.ts`), drawn in the flora materials, plain rock now one of them. A tree near the camera may have 8,000 triangles, its cards included (docs/art-style.md). Grown plants are drawn with the lush spike's foliage shaders (D214, D216, D218), and keep their own generator apart from the spike's, which has moved on since; the two are to be made one. The shade is lit by the sky as `daylight` says hour by hour: Bevy makes the environment light the shading reads once from the generated one and does not follow its brightness or turn, so `skylight` passes them on; until then the shade kept the first frame's light all day, and D216's brighter sky never reached it. | The developers asked for the test scene to meet the art guide (D14): the free low-poly models read as low-poly in real materials, pines as stacked cones and crowns as blobs. SpeedTree's way, branches and leaf cards, is what shipped games use, and the lush spike had shown it running; a generated tree from a model generator is a solid blob, and a hand-modelled one per variant would cost a model each. Cards keep the leaves' shape where triangles could not, and a grown kind is data (D136). Scans have real shape at no cost, and a rock's material is its kind's. Measured back to back with main on the RTX 5090: the world scene 5.7 to 6.7 ms, the lush forest 5.4 to 6.4 before and 5.3 after; on the integrated Radeon both stay where they were, about 200 ms, within the shared machine's noise. |
| D316 | Locomotion clips made, not taken | Tech | Agent | Decided | The idle, walk, jog and sprint the game plays are made with NVIDIA's Kimodo (2026, open weights under a licence that allows commercial use and claims no output), run on this machine from a prompt held to a straight path at the clip's speed, rather than taken from Quaternius's libraries; the best looping stride of the take kept is carried onto our skeleton by world rotation from the T-pose, run in place, the upper limbs' roll handed to the forearm and shin for our skinning (`tools/characters/made_clips.py`, `motion.ts`, `art/animation/made.json`). Other clips stay Quaternius's until one of ours measures better. | The library's jog leans 28 degrees and its sprint 41, two to four times a real runner's, and reads as a hunched charge on our bodies; Kimodo's walk and jog lean 0.4 and 7.8 degrees with the arms close (research/2026-10-14-animation.md, `gait_check.py`). The developers: "get the character dressed and running not so fucking weird." |
| D347 | What people do, made, not taken | Tech | Agent | Decided | Amends D316. The clips a town's people play at their work and talk are made with Kimodo as the locomotion is (`art/animation/made.json`): a still clip looped between its most alike frames, as long as its entry's `loop` allows, a working one (`busy`) in its busiest stretch, each played at the take's own pace; each measured (`gait_check.py`, `clip_check.py`) and looked at on the base body, and kept only if it reads as the work. Made so far: `Talk_Loop`, standing in conversation, for talk and selling. Kimodo follows short plain prompts in the style of its own examples, not long ones naming each limb. | The adversarial review of the market town: "People don't do anything that shows: pairs face each other in a fighting stance (the talk or idle clip on everyone); nobody carries, sells, hammers, sits or leans; stalls have no seller." Quaternius's Idle_Talking_Loop holds the head 15 degrees down and reads as a fighter's crouch on our bodies, as its idle did (D316). |
| D329 | What the player wears | Design | Developers | Decided | A player starts in a leather coat with long sleeves and cloth trousers, with the small pack, barefoot and bald; no cap, which is gone from the game. Trousers are worn in a Legs slot, one of D64's standard slots. Both are made, as all clothing is, from one design of the base person wearing them (`art/gear/dressed-male-outfit`: a dark, matte, plain-fronted leather jacket and plain dark cloth trousers), cut from the base body and baked, leather graded to a dark muted brown and every material kept matte (`skin_piece.py`). The player's body is the base with most of the thin morph (`templates.json`), not a bodybuilder's; its feet are a person's width (6% of the height) and its hands a person's length (10.8%), which every morph keeps. | The developers, 2026-10-15: "Fix the player character. This one is just too goofy, we may need to fix it up, at least it has too large feet if you look at it. The cap is goofy, we can eliminate it. We only need pants and jacket, a bald guy is fine otherwise." |

## Engine and code

Rust and Bevy, terrain, shared state, networking and the game's model.

| # | Topic | Owner | By | Status | Decision | Why, or what closes it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D9 | Terrain representation | Tech | Developers | Decided | Smooth voxels (a density field) on level-of-detail terrain, fully diggable. Structures are separate from the terrain: modular pieces, or a voxel volume of their own on the base's grid. | Blocky voxels don't tile a sphere. The spike rendered it from orbit to ground and dug it without a hitch. Widened on 2026-10-11 so that building in voxels, as Enshrouded does, does not need the terrain itself to hold buildings: a base's blocks keep their own grid and meshing, and the terrain's levels of detail and edits stay untouched. The developers confirmed the same day: building in voxels is fine, as long as the terrain is smooth voxels. |
| D11 | Engine selection criteria | Design | Developers | Decided | Everything in Rust. Within that, the simplest stack that works well with coding agents and lets us reuse existing tech. | Decided by the developers on 2026-10-06, for performance, one language, and compiler-checked agent code, accepting that rebuilding in Rust cost weeks before the game was back to where the Godot demo was (was D27). Replaces the earlier criteria, which only preferred Rust. |
| D12 | Engine | Tech | Agent | Decided | Bevy (0.20, D31) as the framework, with our own voxel terrain ported from the parts of Voxel Tools we need. | Follows from D11. Bevy supplies renderer, entity system, windowing, assets, audio and UI, so we do not write those. Toolchain proven in [research/rust-stack-spike.md](../research/rust-stack-spike.md). Reopen if the terrain port cannot match the Godot numbers. The Godot build was retired on 2026-10-12, before the Rust build had passed the same checks: its numbers stay the bar, in [research/godot-spike.md](../research/godot-spike.md), and its code is in git history. |
| D21 | How terrain edits are stored and synced | Tech | Agent | Decided | As operations (shape, position, size) applied in the server's order by every player, never as voxel data. | Passed the co-op spike test. Add snapshots of edited blocks for late joins once worlds have very many edits. |
| D25 | Performance target | Tech | Developers | Decided | 75 fps, comfortably and always: a frame within 13.3 ms at its 99th percentile at 1080p on the developers' GPU (an RTX 3080) at the high preset, with the frame rate a player's setting (vsync, or a cap). 60 fps at 1080p on a mid-range discrete GPU and a 4-core CPU, as a client of a server holding a thousand players (D52), with dozens of them in view. Every prototype reports frame times on the integrated GPU as a low-end check, measured in the real play loop and not only in scripted runs; there it holds 30 fps at the low preset (D239). Nothing ships on an untested performance assumption. The world must be able to show very many objects and let the player interact with any of them, and we look for ways to make that cheap everywhere. | Performance is a requirement. The developers, 2026-10-15: "performance matters. we will want to have controls over FPS output but generally speaking we would like to comfortably run this game at 75 FPS always." |
| D26 | Where the simulation runs | Tech | Agent | Decided | The threaded world simulation, `terra_sim`, runs on its own thread, at a requested rate, never inside the render frame; the game takes a short lock to read or change it. It is parked with terraforming (D89). Gameplay runs on the fixed tick (D138). | Running it inside the frame dropped the demo to 7 fps at x100. On a thread, x100 (6,000 ticks a second) costs the frame nothing. |
| D28 | How the code is split | Tech | Agent | Decided | Anything that must be the same for every player lives in plain Rust crates that do not depend on Bevy (`terra_core`, `terra_gen`, `terra_voxel`, `terra_build`, `terra_items`, `terra_climate`, `terra_sim`; `terra_net` when networking starts). Only the applications, `terra_game` and the `terra_sandbox` test environment, use Bevy, and `terra_world`, the world on screen the game stands on (D198). | Fast tests with no GPU, a headless host, and Bevy's frequent releases touching one crate. The simulation crate already moved engines unchanged. |
| D29 | How state is shared | Tech | Agent | Decided | World changes are operations applied in the server's order (D21). Gameplay runs on the server's fixed tick (D138); a world simulation runs only on the server and is sent as snapshots (the threaded `terra_sim` is parked with terraforming). Moving things are sent as positions and smoothed. | The simulation is small enough to send, which removes any need for players to compute identical floating-point results. |
| D30 | Maths in shared state | Tech | Agent | Decided | The `libm` crate for sine and similar functions, never the system maths library. A lint rejects the system functions. | Measured: the system library made Windows meshing six times slower; `libm` matched Linux. It also gives the same answer on every platform. |
| D31 | Bevy version policy | Tech | Agent | Decided | Pinned, now to 0.20. Upgrade once the crates we depend on support the new release, or sooner when the developers ask; a dependency that has not caught up is then pinned to an exact commit of its port, or switched off, and returned to a release as soon as one supports the new Bevy. | Bevy changes its API every few months. Moved to 0.20 on 2026-10-12 at the developers' request, a few days after its release, with `bevy-inspector-egui` on the commit of its open port (see `Cargo.toml`); what it took and what it changed is in [research/bevy-0-20-upgrade.md](../research/bevy-0-20-upgrade.md). |
| D32 | Terrain meshing library | Tech | Agent | Decided | The `transvoxel` crate, in `terra_voxel`; port Voxel Tools' mesher if re-meshing on detail changes proves costly. | The crate bakes seam stitching into each mesh. Built and in use since 2026-10-11: a chunk meshes in about 3 ms, within the terrain brief's 6. |
| D33 | Physics | Tech | Agent | Leaning | Avian for rigid bodies. The player and creatures query voxel data directly instead of using collision meshes. | Avian is active, but is untested here; its latest release (0.7) is for Bevy 0.19, not yet 0.20. The voxel-query approach worked in the Godot demo. |
| D34 | Networking library | Tech | Agent | Leaning | Our own messages over `renet`, with a Steam transport added later. No replication framework. | Our model needs only a reliable ordered channel and an unreliable one. Untested here. |
| D35 | UI | Tech | Agent | Leaning | Bevy's own UI for the game; `bevy_egui` for developer tools. | Untested here. Bevy's UI is younger than Godot's; reassess when the game needs real menus. |
| D36 | Coordinates | Tech | Agent | Leaning | One coordinate frame per world, with positions relative to an origin that moves with the player, and 64-bit positions on the server. No solar-system frame and no `big_space`. | With no space (D4), nothing ever spans two worlds, so one frame per world is enough. Was "solar-system coordinates", adopting `big_space` when a second planet existed. |
| D40 | Materials in voxels | Tech | Agent | Decided | Voxels carry a material as well as a shape, from the first version of the terrain. Removing terrain reports the volume of each material taken. | Built and in use since 2026-10-11 (`terra_voxel`): the pickaxe digs stone and ore by volume. The technical reading of "organic harvesting": resources are in the ground and digging yields them. Adding materials to a shape-only terrain later would mean redoing its storage, meshing and edits. Voxel Tools already does this, so there is a design to port. |
| D41 | Generation toolkit | Tech | Agent | Decided | One shared crate, `terra_gen`, for seeds, noise and repeatable random streams, giving the same result on every machine. Built first. Everything generated is a function of the seed and a position; only changes are stored or sent. | Built and in use since 2026-10-10, its outputs pinned and checked on three platforms. Follows from D8. Terrain, materials, scattering, plants and sky all need it, and D21 and D29 depend on every machine generating the same world. |
| D42 | Engine build order | Tech | Agent | Leaning | A small foundation (shared contracts, then the generation toolkit), then four tracks in parallel: terrain; a walkable stand-in world; things on the ground; networking. See [engine.md](engine.md). | A working plan, not a commitment. A stand-in planet computed from the terrain formula let everything except the terrain itself be built before voxels existed. Separate crates keep parallel work from colliding. The voxel terrain is now built (`terra_voxel`) and the game walks on it, so the stand-in world has served its purpose. |
| D45 | Contracts for harvest and water | Tech | Agent | Decided | Every harvest is an operation that returns a yield. Water is asked for by position, not read as one global number. | Built and in use since 2026-10-10 (`terra_core`): operations return a `Yield`, and the ground says where the water is by direction. Keeps D43 open, and water that differs by place possible, at no cost: one sea is a constant answer to the same question pools would answer. |
| D47 | Numerous world objects | Tech | Agent | Leaning | Objects such as trees and rocks are computed from the seed and their location, not stored. Only changes are remembered, as operations. Only nearby ones are drawn, baked together by area, and interaction is by lookup. | Tried in the sandbox: about a million choppable trees on a 2 km planet for 216 KB of worst-case state and 3.4 ms a frame on the RTX 5090. Not yet tried on voxels, with heavier objects, or in motion. See [research/forest-scale-test.md](../research/forest-scale-test.md). |
| D53 | Collision | Tech | — | Open | Required: collision shapes that work with voxel terrain, bases and objects, for players, creatures and aiming. The developers want collision boxes on everything, used for placing things as well as for walking. How is not designed. The leaning in D33 (a physics engine for shapes, with players asking voxel data directly) is untested. | Asked for by the developers on 2026-10-10. The game (`terra_game`) still uses stand-in arithmetic, such as circles on the ground for bodies and trees. See [engine.md](engine.md). |
| D58 | How the game is modelled in code | Tech | Developers | Decided | In ECS. Everything in a world is an entity; its state is components; behaviour is systems. Resources only for what is truly one of a kind. The client and the server both hold the world this way. Rules that every machine must agree on stay in engine-free crates (D28) and are called from systems. | Asked for by the developers on 2026-10-10. It is how Bevy works, it lets a thousand players and their things be processed in bulk, and it keeps D28: the rules are plain functions, the ECS holds the state. What belongs to a player is a component of the player, the builder and inventory included (done on 2026-10-12, [research/ecs-audit.md](../research/ecs-audit.md)). |
| D135 | Loading screen | Tech | Agent | Decided | Every build starts on a loading screen that covers the window until the scene is ready: until everything has arrived and a few quiet frames have passed: files systems ask it to wait for (sounds among them), models put in the world, pictures on screen, everything in view the renderer can draw (its mesh, material and textures prepared, whatever the material), shaders compiled, and work a feature reports itself, such as ground still being meshed. Each is a system reporting to one `Progress` in a `Track` set, so a new kind of work is one system, not a change to the screen. The game clock is held meanwhile, and the screen fades as play begins. It is one module (`terra_game::loading`) with a `Screen` state (loading, playing), so a later title screen enters loading to get the same screen. Scripted runs count their frames from when loading is done. | The developers found the start odd: grey ground and the HUD while models loaded and shaders compiled, with things appearing one by one. A screen over the start, with progress, is what shipped games do; waiting on shader compilation as well as files is what removes the grey ground. Not a loading screen within a world or between worlds, which D4 and D72 rule out. Counting script frames from the end of loading makes scripts independent of how long loading took on a machine. |
| D136 | Kinds of thing as data | Tech | Agent | Decided | Kinds of item, recipe, station and creature are entries in RON files in `assets/data/`, one file per feature, read by the plugin that owns it. Code asks what a kind can do (whether a tool builds, what it cuts, what it shoots) and never names one. The files are read when the game starts, each into a registry: a resource the systems that need it read (`terra_world::data` for the world's, `play::registries` for the game's), with kinds known by their place in it and names resolved to them once, as the file is read. A mistake in a file stops the game, naming the file. Which kinds a server runs on is the server's to send (D51). Every kind is read this way: items, stations, recipes, what the hammer builds, creatures, what grows and what the ground gives. | Mine to decide, to keep D127: the kinds were Rust enums with a `match` per property, so adding one meant code in several files. RON because the game already uses it for saves. Registries rather than files built in, so the content can be swapped as a whole (by a world's theme, by a server, by a file edited while the game runs), which is how data-driven games keep their definitions. |
| D137 | How a player's actions reach the game | Tech | Agent | Decided | Each player has an `Intent` component: the actions they hold and press, the wheel, where they aim, and whether their hands are on a screen. Gameplay reads a player's intent and acts for every player; it never reads the keyboard. This machine writes its own player's intent from its keys and mouse; a server will write everyone else's from what their games send. What a player carries, learns and is told names them (`Belongings::of`, `Did`, `Told`). | Mine to decide, for D51: a server runs the same gameplay for every player and a client predicts its own with it, which only works if input is per-player data. It is the pattern of leafwing-input-manager's `ActionState`, Overwatch's command frames and Unreal's player input. |
| D138 | The game's tick | Tech | Agent | Decided | Gameplay runs at a fixed rate, 64 ticks a second, in Bevy's `FixedUpdate`, in ordered stages: players act on their intent, the world answers and goes on, what happened is settled, presses are spent. What is shown (animation, sound, screens, the camera) runs every frame and only reads. Intent is read before a frame's ticks and a press lasts until a tick has seen it. Moving bodies are shown between their last two ticks, so movement is as smooth as the frame rate. | Mine to decide, for D51: a server ticks at a fixed rate, and a client predicting its own player has to run the same ticks. Fixed-rate simulation apart from presentation, with interpolation, is how shipped server-authoritative games and Bevy's own fixed-timestep examples work. Measured: walking, the player covers the same distance every frame. |
| D145 | Simulation level of detail | Tech | Agent | Decided | Creatures are simulated by how near the nearest player is: within 64 m every tick; within 256 m they move every tick but think once in eight, taking turns; beyond, they wait where they are. Each has a `Detail`, worked out every tick (`play::lod`), which the systems that simulate it ask. Other kinds of thing join it as they need to. The distances are a first guess, to be measured. | Mine to decide, for D127 and a thousand players on one server: ticks go where someone can see the result. Simulation or AI LOD is how shipped open worlds keep many actors, as Unreal's significance manager does. Moving every tick at reduced detail keeps what is seen smooth; thinking less often is what saves. |
| D184 | How far we change Bevy | Tech | Agent | Decided | Extend first: our own systems, components, material extensions and render steps beside Bevy's. Replace one of Bevy's plugins or shaders when a measurement shows its version won't do, as our skinning shader replaces Bevy's. Fork a Bevy crate only as a last resort, through Cargo's patch, with the change kept small and written here so it can be carried to each release or offered upstream. Techniques Bevy lacks are ported from an engine that does them well (D196). | The developers asked whether we are locked to Bevy's limits (2026-10-12): we are not, but the deeper the change, the more each Bevy release costs us (0.20 moved every shader to WESL). Bevy's character limits are in [research/bevy-character-capabilities.md](../research/bevy-character-capabilities.md); the likeliest to need replacing is joint matrices computed per skinned mesh rather than per skeleton, if the crowd measurement asks for it ([characters.md](characters.md)). |
| D196 | When Bevy falls short | Tech | Developers | Decided | Find the engine or public tool that does it well and reimplement its version on Bevy: ported from its source where the licence lets an MIT project take the code (MIT, BSD, Apache, zlib: Godot, Voxel Tools, O3DE, Jolt), and from its documentation, papers and talks where it does not (Unreal, anything GPL). Not an invention of our own, and not a change of engine. How deep into Bevy the change goes is D184. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-12, after asking whether Haxe and Heaps would spare us the character and terrain problems: "if bevy isnt good at something, our best solution is to find a version of it in an engine that is, and reimplement it, ideally from source if we can". Those problems were mostly not Bevy's (stale clips, a rig whose twist bones moved no skin, a crate's seam bug), and a smaller engine has the same gaps. A shipped implementation's source carries the edge cases a description leaves out. |
| D198 | The game in layers | Tech | Agent | Decided | `terra_game` is three plugin layers, each standing only on those before it: the world (data, light, the day, what grows, the tick, levels of detail, on the planet, terrain and the start), the walker (a body this machine steers, its camera, controls and options), and gameplay (everything a player does). They are bundled as `GamePlugins`; the full game turns on all three, and a run can leave layers out, as the sandbox's `walk` does. A layer never needs a system or resource above it to run: it orders by system sets, publishes small types the layer above fills in (`Hands`, `ArriveAt`, `WarpTo`), adds what it owns to new entities with observers, and takes what may be missing as optional. The start is the world's, chosen from the planet, not the build's. The world is its own crate, `terra_world`, under `terra_game`, which holds the walker and gameplay; the world owns the types the game meets it through (`Footprint`, `Viewpoint`, flora's items by name). See [tech.md](tech.md#layers-of-the-game-d198). | The developers on 2026-10-13: "we want modules pluggable and mostly large enough chunks i.e. not 'microservices' equivalent. And we want main game to still do pretty much entire thing, but we are doing it so its better to test and build modules in isolation", and "lets make sure this also is like a proper bevy/ecs pattern". Walking through the forest needed all fifty of the game's plugins, since the build held the start and every screen was read by name. Plugin groups, system sets, observers and run conditions are Bevy's own ways to compose plugins. Checked by tracing the game before and after through the same keys: the player's state agreed on every frame; only creatures at reduced detail think on other ticks, since entities were spawned in another order. |
| D239 | Quality presets, and what the integrated GPU must hold | Tech | Agent | Decided | Three presets, low, medium and high (`terra_world::quality`), chosen when the game starts from the GPU, an integrated one low and any other high, as shipped games pick their first preset from the adapter; `--quality` or the options menu's Graphics (automatic or a preset) overrides it. High is the look as made. Medium: a 2048 shadow map in three cascades to 200 m, the haze in 24 steps, relief in 8 layers, details and grass at 0.8 and 0.75 of their reach and density, buildings' finest detail casting no shadow and their middle detail's cast by a copy only the sun sees. Low adds the world drawn at three fifths of the screen's width and height and scaled up by a camera of its own, on its own render layer and standing where the main one does, sharpened first with AMD's contrast-adaptive sharpening as FidelityFX's upscaler finishes (`upscale`); two cascades to 60 m with the hardware's 2×2 filtering; no shade in corners, contact shadows, haze shafts or bloom; flat relief; the ground drawn cheaper (D307); the air's tables at half resolution; detail at half its reach (`DetailScale`, D230) and half the grass. The integrated GPU's target is 30 frames a second at 1080p shown, at the low preset: a median under 33.3 ms. | D25 sets 60 fps on a mid-range discrete GPU and asks for the integrated GPU's frame times without saying what they must be; 30 fps at a scaled resolution or a low preset is the industry's low end, as consoles' performance modes and Steam Deck targets are. Measured on the Ryzen 9 9950X3D's two-unit Radeon at 1080p: the yard from 187 to 25 ms, the village from 288 to 34, just over, the world and the forest from 324 and 376 to 71 and 91, which still miss on their foliage; the high preset on the RTX 5090 is unchanged ([research/integrated-gpu.md](../research/integrated-gpu.md)). |
| D288 | Foliage shaded only where it is in front | Tech | Agent | Decided | Leaves, grass, litter and the plants that sway, all cut by their pictures' alpha, write no depth in the main pass after the depth prepass has written it (`grown::test_depth_early`): a fragment shader that discards keeps the GPU from testing depth before it runs unless the pass leaves depth alone, so every card behind the front ones was shaded and thrown away. The depth test is unchanged, so what is drawn is the same. Unreal's masked materials after its early depth pass do the same. | Foliage was most of the integrated GPU's frame at the low preset (D239): hiding the grown trees' leaves took the world from 95 to 59 ms. With this, on the Radeon at low, the world's main pass went from 60 to 39 to 42 ms and its frame from 95 to 71 to 77, the lush forest's main pass from 108 to 55 to 60 and its frame from 162 to 105 to 117; pictures from the same place match. |
| D307 | The low preset drawn as integrated GPUs need | Tech | Agent | Decided | Amends D239. What the low preset draws is cut where an integrated GPU spends its frame, as shipped games' low presets do, measured pass by pass. The ground is another build of its shader (`GROUND_LOW`): each layer's share worked out first, as a splat map holds them, and only the two that show most sampled (a top-two splat, as Far Cry 5's terrain keeps a few layers a texel), the floor's litter, needles, grass and moss settled by height and the rest laid over as the high preset lays them; each projected along the one or two axes the surface faces most (Quilez's biplanar mapping), not three; flat in its layers' own colours past 45 to 75 m, which is what their smallest mips show there; and without the soil's stones or the finer fraying of the cover map. The high preset's ground is unchanged. | The developers asked for the low preset to meet D239's 30 frames a second in the world, the forest, the village, a house going up and the town on the Ryzen 9 9950X3D's two-unit Radeon. The ground cost the world's main pass about 15 ms of its 46 there: with this the world went from 84 and 86 ms to 74 and 67, its main pass from 45 and 48 to 33 and 29, on a machine shared with other agents' builds (load 30 to 90 on 32 cores); pictures from the same place match but for the floor's finest detail ([research/integrated-gpu.md](../research/integrated-gpu.md)). |
| D283 | Mesh slabs start at 64 MiB and double | Tech | Agent | Decided | Bevy keeps meshes in shared slabs of GPU memory (its mesh allocator); a full slab is copied into a bigger one, after which every mesh in it is prepared and batched again. Ours start at 64 MiB and double up to Bevy's 512 MiB (`shell`), three growths a slab rather than a dozen from Bevy's 1 MiB by half again. | The `town300` street walk on the 5090, back to back at load 8 to 15: with Bevy's slabs the 99th percentile was 24 to 32 ms with up to 9 hitches, the trace showing `allocate_and_free_meshes` up to 52 ms and the bind groups and indirect buffers rebuilt after it at up to 27 ms; with these, 12 to 17 ms in three runs of four and no hitch, for 25% more memory held in slabs (3.1 to 3.8 GiB in the town). |
| D322 | Mountains, lakes and water | Tech | Agent | Decided | The world's ground gains a mountain term set by a theme's numbers (relief, ridge sharpness, erosion): ridged fBm damped by its own slope (Quilez's derivative noise), and gullies cut per point along the downhill gradient (Runevision's erosion filter, worked from its write-up, its code being MPL-2.0); bare rock above 40°, scree at 24 to 38° with a cliff within 45 m above, gravel in the gullies' beds. Lakes are the generated ground's depressions filled to their spill points by Priority-Flood on a coarse grid, before edits, answered through `terra_core::Water` (D45). Water is drawn as one layer after the opaque pass, a medium that absorbs by depth read from the depth prepass (Beer's law, ported from boujie's MIT water shader for Godot, D196), with foam at the shore from depth, a wet band on the ground above it and ripples by the climate's wind; for now it mirrors the skyline round the lake as the land's rules make it (forest, rock and snow by height, hazed), taken once at the start; a planar reflection at a quarter of the screen that draws only the cheap levels of detail and the sky, as Unreal's planar reflection takes an LOD factor, is still to come. Far peaks are the octree's own coarse chunks, shaded past the last cascade by a horizon map baked with each. | Mountains need a kilometre of relief before the lapse rate makes a tree line (the world's is 150 m, about 1 °C), and erosion has to work per point because the world is generated where you stand. Priority-Flood is the standard way to fill a height field's basins and gives engine.md's basin filled to its own level in one pass. Bevy's screen-space reflections need the deferred renderer and ours is forward, and a still mountain lake reads by what it reflects. `bevy_water` does not support Bevy 0.20. Measured on the first steps, and changed with a reason if they fail. See [demos.md](demos.md#1-a-mountain-lake). |
| D331 | The land's rules in one engine-free crate, and a place's latitude its own | Tech | Agent | Decided | `terra_land`, free of Bevy (D28): the world's ground as generated (`ground`, which `terra_world::Planet` now builds from) and its climate taken together, which neither knows of the other. The world's **frame** (`frame::Frame`) is the axis the sky turns about, turned as `SkyPath` always turned it (the morning sun where the art was lit, the planet's own start at 40°); every point's latitude follows from it, and so does how its ground leans toward the equator (`Place::sunward`, D323's facing). The sun stays fixed in the world's sky; how high it stands, the light keyed to that and the moon are taken where the camera is (`SkyPath::seen_from`), so a place 5 km north on the 30 km world sees its own lower sun. A slope's sunlight is level ground's that much nearer the equator, warming the air by 0.008 °C a W/m² of difference and melting its snow; a place's growing season, and the tree line where it stops holding trees, are Paulsen and Körner's (a mean of 6.4 °C over at least 94 days above 0.9 °C, `terra_climate::Season`). `cargo xtask fields` draws the field maps (D326) over it, with no GPU. | The demos need each place's latitude, not the start's, and the climate bent by the ground (docs/demos.md); the field maps must see the same land as the game without the engine, so the ground's construction and the frame moved below Bevy. Measured with the start's climate: the tree line at 1,668 m on level ground at 40°, 1,251 m at 45°, 393 m at 55°, none at 60°; on a 20° slope facing the pole about 60 m lower at 40° and 90 m at 55°; on 20 June the snow line at 2,400 m at 40°. |
| D335 | Woods and snow by the land's rules, carried on the ground | Tech | Agent | Decided | On a world with mountains the land round the start is worked out ahead (`terra_land::round::Round`, 16 km across): its lakes on a 10 m grid, the growing season over its latitudes, heights and leanings (every 0.1° and 25 m), and the snow lying on the clock's date from the folded past (every 0.25° and 50 m), each blended between its points. Trees stand by `terra_land::woods::stand`: closed forest where the growing season is a degree warmer than the tree line's 6.4 °C, thinning and shrinking to krummholz half a degree colder than it, none past 45° or on rock or water, a few on scree and gravel; Norway spruce on shaded slopes, Scots pine on sunny ones. Within 600 m of the start each is a grown conifer, out to 4.5 km a far billboard in the far forest's shader (D273), merged in 250 m squares, past that the ground's own dark canopy tint. The ground carries, per vertex, its loose stones, snow depth, bare rock or woods and its height over the nearest lake (terra_world `terrain::lying`), and its shader draws scree, gravel, snow on what faces up, the canopy far off and a wet band at a lake's edge. | The developers want forested slopes and high peaks (docs/demos.md §1); D313 has species' niches decide what grows. A tree line drawn from the growing season is a band that rises and falls with facing, never a contour. Working seasons and snow ahead keeps tens of thousands of trees and every vertex to a lookup: a season is a year of days, a fold most of a year. |
| D323 | The ground bends the climate | Tech | Agent | Decided | Amends D314. `terra_climate::Place` carries how the ground faces the sun and how much drifting snow it catches, worked out by the world from its terrain (the slope's aspect; Winstral's Sx, the steepest upwind slope within 100 m against the prevailing wind), so the climate crate never reads terrain: a sunny slope is warmer, a lee hollow takes more of what falls as snow and keeps it longer, a windward rise less. A place's latitude is read from where it is, not a constant. | Tree lines rise on sunny slopes; lee slopes hold about twice the snow of windward ones, and late snow patches recur in the same places every year ([research](../research/2026-10-15-biome-demos.md#tundra)). Without the ground the fields lay the same snow on a ridge as in a gully. Keeping terrain out of the climate crate keeps D28's split and its tests. See [demos.md](demos.md#2-a-tundra). |
| D337 | The tundra found on the land, and the snow the ground catches | Tech | Agent | Decided | Amends D323. The tundra is found by a query on the land's rules (`terra_land::tundra`, `--site tundra` for the sandbox and `cargo xtask fields`), not placed: over a 60 km grid at 100 m round the planet's start, level ground (under 7° and 40 m of rise and fall within 300 m) whose growing season is 0.8 to 3 °C colder than the tree line's 6.4, whose year's mean is under −2 °C (permafrost), a closed forest 0.5 to 3 km off, best with the forest's edge 1.5 km away, small lakes round it and near the start. A place's catch of drifting snow (`Place::catch`) is a share of open level ground's, from Winstral's Sx over 100 m against the climate's prevailing wind (`terra_land::drift`): 1 + 1.2 tanh(Sx/8°) in a lee, to 2.2, and 1 + 0.75 tanh(Sx/8°) exposed, to 0.25; the fold adds what falls times it. The land round a place (`Round`) works it out on its lakes' 10 m grid; its snow table is folded at catches of 0.25, 1 and 2.2 and blended between, and the climate maps and samples take the same catch. | The developers on 2026-10-15: "i would like to see more of a tundra effect", in the starting world's own north where its climate puts it (demos.md, question 3, its recommendation). On the 30 km world the query finds a fell plateau 10.5 km north and 15.5 km west of the start at 966 m and 50.6° (growing season 5.6 °C, year −6.1 °C), the forest's edge 1.9 km south, as Hardangervidda is a tundra plateau at 1,100 to 1,200 m; the lowlands north of it are mountain or polar desert by these rules. Lee slopes hold about twice the snow of windward ones ([research](../research/2026-10-15-biome-demos.md#tundra)); the field maps show it. |
| D338 | The mountain lake's open air: the eye opened, the air's sky in the water, trees off the shore | Tech | Agent | Decided | On a world whose views reach kilometres (D334), by day out in the open (`OpenOverhead` over 0.55), the camera is set up to 1.5 stops brighter than the hour's key; in a forest, on the lush forest's world and by night, the key. Lakes mirror the air's own sky (`AirPicture`, exposed as the sky is), and the slopes round them in its horizon's light, not the painted sky's two colours; their ripples are half as wide and calmer. A tree's foot stands 0.4 m over the surface of any lake within two of its 10 m cells. Where the ground's vertices carry the snow (D335), the climate map's snow fades out before its edge. No cost to frame time. | The adversarial review scored the lake 4/10, its first gap the noon sky reading as dusk. Swept one thing at a time on the shore view at 13:00 on 10 June: the air's scale, its dust and its dust's height changed the sky's colour but not its brightness (dust 3 km deep turned it grey-lilac); 1.5 stops brighter took the sky from 0.17 to 0.53 (the references' 0.73), the frame from 0.24 to 0.41 (0.39) and the far land under the sky from 0 to 0.44 (0.45). The exposures were tuned under Sons of the Forest's canopy. The water's lilac was the painted sky's horizon at a fixed brightness where the sky drawn is the air's; the trees in a metre of water stood in dry cells whose ground lay under the lake's level; the far ridges' white smear was the climate map's edge, which answers for everything past it. |
| D341 | The heath, patterned ground and the climate's wind | Tech | Agent | Decided | Open ground past the trees is a heath of five communities by exposure and wetness (`terra_land::heath`, docs/demos.md §2): fellfield on scoured rises (Sx under about −6°) and the coldest ground, lichen heath on the dry, dwarf-shrub heath where winter's snow lies over it (the lee, level ground some), tussock sedge from a wetness of about 0.45, moss carpet past 0.75 and at pools' edges; wetness from a hollow under the ground within 30 m, level ground, water within a few metres, permafrost's held water and drainage patches of noise some 60 m across; the heath holds the ground as the season cools past the tree line and the trees thin, `smoothstep(−1.5, 0.6, colder) × (1 − trees)`. It is worked out on the land's 10 m grid round a place, blended over neighbouring cells twice, and given to the ground as a two-layer picture (`planet::LandMap`) it draws as its scanned layers retinted, the dwarf shrubs coloured by their own year (`dwarf_birch`) and the sedge by grass's; the cover's blades are its sedge, and the planet's grass and flora give way to it. Where the land says the year's mean is under −2 °C the ground is patterned (`terra_voxel::Patterned`, laid by `Land::new` from the climate's temperatures): ice-wedge polygons a slice of a jittered 3D Voronoi 22 m apart, troughs 0.4 m deep and 2 m wide, rims 0.15 m, middles 0.2 m low and a fifth 0.8 m, as pools found on a 2 m grid over 1.6 km round the place (`Round::pools`) and drawn as one surface. The one wind (D255) is the climate's at the camera (`terra_world::wind::WorldWind`), every 0.002 days of the clock, its strength the speed over the climate's mean, 0.25 to 2.2, laid over each material's own sway. | The developers on 2026-10-15: "i would like to see more of a tundra effect". Real tundra is a mosaic of these communities by wind and wetness, with ice-wedge polygons about 30 m across under half a metre of relief and pools along them ([research](../research/2026-10-15-biome-demos.md#tundra)); Far Cry 5 and Horizon place by such fields. A tundra's variants (D182): five communities by four seasons by how exposed and how wet, about 40 patches a player tells apart, broken by pool fields, boulders, snowbeds and spruce islands; a new cold realm is new species in the same communities. |
| D342 | Trees at their limit stand in islands | Tech | Agent | Decided | Amends D335. A tree's growing season is warmed or chilled for it by its place's microsite (`terra_land::woods::Microsites`, `stand_in`): 0.5 °C × tanh(Sx / 8°), warmer in the lee and colder on a scoured rise, and up to 0.9 °C either way in islands of noise some 220 m across, so past the closed forest the trees stand in islands thinning and stunting onto the open heath, in the field maps, the woods drawn and the heath alike. | The forest-tundra (*Waldtundra*) is islands of stunted trees in sheltered ground, not a line (Holtmeier, *Mountain Timberlines*, 2009; [research](../research/2026-10-15-biome-demos.md#tundra)); on the tundra's level plateau the tree line had followed the latitude, a straight band a kilometre wide. |
| D345 | The heath's dwarf shrubs, and blown snow | Tech | Agent | Decided | Amends D325 and D341. Past the trees the flora grows dwarf shrubs (`Grows::Heath`, "Dwarf shrub" in flora.ron: the berry bush's cards, 0.18 to 0.45 m) within 110 m of the start on a 1.7 m grid, as often as the heath's dwarf-shrub share (×1.3) and a little among its lichen, in clumps by noise; their leaves turn and fall as the dwarf birch's year says, which names the berry cards (so the berry bushes, bilberry, turn too). Blown snow is smoke's cards (`smoke::Burning::Snow`): up to 64 emitters on the deepest patches lying within 160 m of the start, on a 9 m grid, as thick as the snow is deep, carried at the wind's own speed, life 2.6 s, rising 0.18 m/s, 0.5 to 2.4 m wide; it lifts from 4.5 m/s and is thickest by 9. Smoke's north is the world's frame's. Amends D341: a polygon's pool stands as water once 0.35 m deep and 8 m² (the low middles a hand deep are wet hollows), drawn on its own cells only, so no sheet of water lies over the troughs round it; and the heath holds the ground under the forest-tundra's islands of trees too, only a closed forest a degree and a half warmer than the tree line keeping its own floor. | The developers on 2026-10-15: "i would like to see more of a tundra effect"; demos.md §2 asks for dwarf shrubs on the heath and snow lifting off patches above a speed. Loose snow starts to drift at about 5 m/s and blows in sheets by 10 (snow transport thresholds, Li and Pomeroy 1997). |
| D325 | Smoke, sparks and blown snow | Tech | Agent | Decided | Drawn as instanced soft cards facing the camera, on our own material, moved on the GPU from each emitter's seed and the climate's wind, as fragments are drawn instanced (D305), with no particle dependency: a chimney's column, a cooking fire, a forge's sparks, snow lifting off patches above a wind speed. Capped by the preset. | There is no particle system, and the few kinds a place needs are cheaper kept on our own path, in step with each Bevy release, than a dependency. If we come to need much more, `bevy_hanabi` is the candidate, checked against the pinned Bevy first. See [demos.md](demos.md#3-a-living-town). |
| D332 | A frame-rate limit, and one frame budget | Tech | Agent | Decided | The options menu has a frame-rate limit beside vsync: off, 30, 60, 75, 120, 144 or the screen's own refresh rate as Bevy reports it, applied with vsync off (choosing one turns vsync off), stored in `settings.txt` as `frame_limit`. It is a frame limiter as shipped games and RivaTuner have (`terra_world::pacing`): at the end of the render world's frame, after the frame is presented, it sleeps until the next frame is due, the system's sleep for all but the last millisecond and a spin for that, and a frame is due a period after the last was due, so a slightly late frame is made up and a hitch restarts the beat. Ported in outline from bevy_framepace (MIT or Apache 2.0), which has no release for Bevy 0.20, rather than taken as a dependency; Bevy 0.20 has no limiter. Only the game sets it, so benchmarks and scripted runs stay uncapped; there is no command-line option, as there is none for vsync. The frame budget is one constant, `terra_world::debug::FRAME_BUDGET_MS`, 1000/75 ms (D25); the benchmark reads it from the sandbox's report rather than keeping its own. | The developers, 2026-10-15: "we will want to have controls over FPS output but generally speaking we would like to comfortably run this game at 75 FPS always" (D25). The budget was 16.7 ms in three places that had to agree. |
| D340 | mimalloc as the global allocator | Tech | Agent | Decided | The sandbox, which is the game's program, allocates with mimalloc (the `mimalloc` crate, MIT, Microsoft's) rather than the system allocator, as engines replace it (Unreal ships mimalloc and TBB options). | The game's workers make and free hundreds of megabytes of section meshes, face caches and base copies while the main and render threads run; on Windows the system heap's lock made them wait 20 to 30 ms behind the workers, frames with nothing of their own to do (the castle tower's fall: Bevy preparing no new asset 24 ms, the main thread 30 ms). With mimalloc the tower's range had no frame over 26.7 ms in three runs at loads of 19 to 54 on 32 cores, where it had one to six. |
| D354 | The town's place found on the land, its fields cleared of the forest | Tech | Agent | Decided | Serves D350's horizon. The market town is found by a query on the land's rules (`terra_land::town`, `--site town` for the sandbox and `cargo xtask fields`), not placed by hand: over a grid at 50 m within 9 km of the planet's start, level ground (under 5° at its middle and 12 m of rise and fall within 120 m, on soil, no lake) whose growing season is at least 3 °C warmer than the tree line's 6.4, for its fields; closed forest within 700 m past its fields on at least 55% of 32 ways out; the highest ground within 7 km at least 900 m over it and 200 m over the tree line, the skyline from the square at least 5° high. Best: level, wooded round, the range high over it and round much of it, near the start. The world's start stands 330 m beside it, on the most level ground away from the range, for the base, the village and the herd. Below the tree line the rules stand closed forest on all level ground, so a town has cleared its own (`Clearing`): fields 300 m round it with an edge that wanders 22% in and out over 160 m and thins over 70 m, and 170 m round the start; the land round the place (`Round::stand`) keeps no trees there, so the grown trees, the far forest's billboards and the ground's canopy agree. The woods are grown round the town, not the start, and its ground cover is laid over the world's within its reach. | The developers on 2026-10-15: "in the distance it should either still be a town, or have some sort of fauna on the horizon / mountains in the horizon. We want to make this visually appealing." 300 m from the small planet's start the town looked out on empty grassland to a bare horizon. On the 30 km world the query finds a valley floor 2.2 km east and 2.1 km north of the start at 25 m (growing season 11.4 °C), the range rising to 2,779 m 3.9 km north, the skyline to 36°. |

## Tools and workflow

Checks, developer tools, languages and the repository.

| # | Topic | Owner | By | Status | Decision | Why, or what closes it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D23 | Development workflow | Tech | Agent | Decided | One Cargo workspace. `cargo test` runs everything with no GPU. Screenshots come from the Linux build under WSL's software renderer; performance is measured on the Windows build, cross-compiled from WSL, and natively on macOS. Toolchain, Bevy, Blender (`tools/blender/version`) and all dependencies are pinned. Debug builds keep line numbers only. | Each step was exercised in the toolchain check except macOS. Full debug information made a Windows build too large to load. |
| D37 | Repository tooling | Tech | Agent | Decided | One Cargo workspace at the root with a pinned compiler. `cargo xtask check` is the gate for every change, run by a git hook before each commit (`.githooks`, switched on by any `cargo xtask` task). CI can run it on Linux, macOS and Windows, but only when started by hand: it is off until we need it. `cargo xtask doctor` checks a machine, and `cargo xtask win` runs a Windows build on the GPU from WSL. `AGENTS.md` holds the instructions for every coding agent; `CLAUDE.md` only points to it. Agent skills live in `.agents/skills`, with `.claude/skills` linking to it, so every coding agent finds them. | Two developers on different platforms, and agents writing the code, need one command that says whether a change is acceptable. Tasks are written in Rust so they run the same everywhere. CI turned off on 2026-10-12: "im not sure CI is really adding much value rn btw, worth disabling it til we need it". |
| D46 | A sandbox for experiments | Tech | Developers | Decided | `terra_sandbox` is the test environment: it launches the game, and holds the testbeds (scenes that try one thing) and the developer tools that need the renderer, switched on from the command line. The game's code, and the shell every build runs under (window, launch options, scripted runs, frame timing, the remote protocol), are `terra_game` (D133). | The developers wanted somewhere to experiment that is neither gameplay nor terrain. The game then grew inside it as its `play` experiment, and the shell with it; on 2026-10-12 both moved to `terra_game`, leaving the sandbox what it was meant to be. Was Leaning, with whether the sandbox's parts become the game's shell undecided. |
| D69 | QA for agents | Tech | Agent | Decided | The game's QA is reachable by our coding agents: scripted runs, and live over Bevy's remote protocol through a command line, `tools/sandbox/remote.ts`, with methods the game defines (press keys, step the clock, wait on what the run says, read a digest of the state, set a scene up). Checks read what is true from the game rather than the screen. An MCP server only if a client that cannot run commands needs one. Off in release builds. | Asked for by the developers on 2026-10-10. On 2026-10-12 they left the shape to me. Every engine's agent tooling converges on the same core, and Unity now builds a CLI with MCP as one client of it; a CLI costs an agent no context until used and composes with scripts, and the work that matters is in the game's methods, not the transport. See [development.md](development.md#checking-the-game-without-a-person-qa-for-agents) and [research/agent-debugging-tools.md](../research/agent-debugging-tools.md). |
| D71 | Developer tools | Tech | Developers | Decided | Tools that help refine each system, scriptable as well as usable by hand, and easy to use: a developer can read what one shows at a glance without knowing how it works, and it explains itself where it is used. Those that need the renderer are testbeds in `terra_sandbox`, everything else in `tools/<name>/` (D133), each started from `cargo xtask`. The model workbench is built: it lines models up side by side against simple terrain, held and worn, through the same animation. Still wanted: a way for a developer to review models and leave notes the agent reads. | Asked for by the developers on 2026-10-10; review added on 2026-10-11, to test models and let a person QA them. On 2026-10-13: "make our debug tools easy to use. that's a requirement". Built and in use since 2026-10-11: the workbench, the yard, the remote, and the prop, icon, character and Tripo tools. See [development.md](development.md#developer-tools). |
| D77 | Logs and frame-time readout | Tech | Developers | Decided | Every build has a frame-time readout on screen at all times, judged against the D25 budget, with a graph of the last 240 frames' work, a bar a frame against the budget line, and more on F3: a graph each for game CPU, render CPU and GPU, frame times, render time per pass, entities, memory. A frame's work is the longest of game CPU, render CPU and GPU, since they run side by side; with vsync on, time between frames only reflects the screen. The log keeps engine chatter out and records a summary of frame times once a minute and a warning when frames stutter (over twice the budget), so every session leaves a record of how it ran. | Asked for by the developers so performance budgets can be kept. Built in `terra_world` (`debug.rs`); `--clean` leaves the readout off pictures. The graph added on 2026-10-13: a red line at 77 fps was hard to read, since its colour came from a figure it did not show. Budgets for parts of a frame (terrain, plants, players) come when those parts exist. See [development.md](development.md#logs-and-the-frame-time-readout). |
| D81 | Debug menu | Tech | Developers | Decided | An in-game debug menu, switched on by a flag at launch and opened with the backtick key, for debugging tasks such as warping the player. Absent unless the flag is given. | Asked for by the developers on 2026-10-10 (the key was "maybe"). Should share its commands with QA for agents (D69), so anything the menu does an agent can do. See [development.md](development.md#debug-menu). |
| D82 | Token stats | Tech | Agent | Decided | Each developer's Claude token use on this repository is recorded by a Cloudflare Worker with D1, fed by a script that reads Claude Code's transcripts and runs from a Claude Code hook after each turn. Totals are on a public page. A session counts if it started in the repository or a worktree. Each message is stored once, keyed by its API id, with totals per person, day and model. Shared key; no accounts. Tripo credits count in the cost too: our Tripo scripts record each task's credits in a local file the same script sends, stored once by task id; API credits are priced at their list price and Studio credits at the subscription's price per credit. | Asked for by the developers on 2026-10-10, at the least possible cost and never counting twice. Workers and D1 on the free plan cost nothing and stop rather than bill at a limit. Transcripts repeat a message many times, including in resumed conversations under other ids and on other days, so totals per day or per conversation would double count; per message they cannot. Plain Node, no dependencies, so it runs anywhere Claude Code does. The worker reports its errors to Sentry with `@sentry/cloudflare`, its one runtime dependency, since otherwise a failing worker goes unseen until someone looks at the page. See [tools/token-stats](../tools/token-stats/README.md). |
| D121 | Languages | Tech | Developers | Decided | Code is Rust or TypeScript. The game and anything it shares is Rust; tooling outside the game (scripts, CLIs, the token stats' worker) is TypeScript on Node, run directly with no build step. Short shell scripts are fine as glue (launching a program, chaining commands); anything with real logic is TypeScript or Rust. No new Python or other languages, except code that must run inside a program that only speaks Python, such as Blender scripts. Python no longer used is deleted rather than ported. | Said by the developers on 2026-10-11: they did not want one-off scripts in assorted languages, and the project already needs Node for Cloudflare and Rust for Bevy. Two languages are fewer to know, set up and check. TypeScript over Rust for tooling: it needs no compile and adds no dependencies to the game's workspace, which suits glue around services that change under us. |
| D133 | Repository layout | Tech | Developers | Decided | Each kind of thing has one home. Runtime assets the game loads in `assets/` at the root; the source art the pipeline makes them from in `art/`; engine-free rules in their crates (D28); the game's Bevy code in `terra_game`; testbeds and engine-side developer tools in `terra_sandbox`, on top of the game; everything that runs outside the game, the asset pipeline included, in `tools/<name>/`; repository tasks in `xtask`. A spike holds only throwaway work, answers one question, and is deleted once its research note is written; anything something else depends on moves out of it first. Where a new thing goes is in [research/repo-layout.md](../research/repo-layout.md#proposed-layout). | Things began as spikes or experiments, became load-bearing and were never moved: the game grew inside the sandbox, the character pipeline inside spikes, and its source art in an ignored scratch folder of one worktree. The layout follows Rust workspaces (rust-analyzer, Bevy), multiplayer games in Rust (Veloren) and the industry's split of source art from runtime assets. Agreed by the developers on 2026-10-12, who also had the Godot builds deleted (D12). |
| D183 | Builds in worktrees | Tech | Agent | Decided | A new worktree's `target/` is seeded from the main checkout's compiled dependencies before it first builds: `cargo xtask seed`, started by a Claude Code hook when a session or subagent starts in a worktree with no build. Only dependencies are seeded, never our own crates, and it follows Cargo's locks, so a build started meanwhile waits for it. Their `.rlib`, `.rmeta` and `.so` files are hard links to the main checkout's, since rustc and the linker only ever replace those with new files; fingerprints, dep-info and build-script output, which are rewritten in place, are copied. No compiler cache and no build folder shared between checkouts. | Every new worktree built all of Bevy, 5 to 7 minutes; seeded, it builds in about a minute. sccache missed every crate built on a build script, since those embed the worktree's path, so it saved nothing. A shared `build.build-dir` let one worktree take another's edited code, since Cargo judges our crates fresh by file times. Copied, the seed was 10.5 GB of real disk per worktree on ext4, which cannot clone files, and ten subagents at once held over 130 GB (2026-10-13); linked, it is 0.2 GB. Measured in [research/worktree-builds.md](../research/worktree-builds.md). |
| D185 | How performance is measured | Tech | Agent | Decided | `cargo xtask bench` runs named scenes (`yard`, `world`, `forest`, `forest-walk`, `village` and `village-bare` (fifty buildings on the yard, and the same view bare, D213), building at scale (`town300` walked down a street, `town300-rise`, `town300-fly` and `town300-bare`, three hundred buildings; `castle` and `castle-field`, one base of 140,000 cubes; `far`, three hundred bases out to 2 km; on the yard at a 30 km radius, cameras gliding between `--vantage`s with `--glide`), and `walk`, 40 seconds through the lush forest on a scripted `--route` with the world and walker alone, looking up into the canopy once halfway, the heaviest view), each a scripted run at 17:00, built for release and drawn offscreen at 1920x1080 with one frame in flight: 240 frames to settle, then each scene's own count timed (600, or 2,400 for `walk`), three runs, reported as the middle run's median and 99th percentile and the worst frame of any. Each scene must hold to D25's frame budget on the machine it runs on, vsync off and no frame-rate limit: its 99th percentile under it, at most 1% of frames over, no hitch (a frame over twice it), and the game's own work no more than 15% slower in its last third than its first; the task fails if one misses. The GPU's time is the passes that ran in the frame last timed, so a pass that ran once is not counted ever after; each run also says how long the render world worked on a frame and then waited for the GPU, which shows what the passes' times miss, the sun's shadow maps among them. Results are kept in [research/benchmarks.csv](../research/benchmarks.csv); a median more than 10% slower than the last recorded on the same GPU, size and build is a regression to look into. The task notes a busy machine. On macOS a scripted run keeps itself out of App Nap, which slowed hidden runs threefold after 40 s. Benchmarks stream the ground as play does. Scenes stay short: a benchmark is run often. See [development.md](development.md#measuring-performance). | Asked for by the developers on 2026-10-12: "some sort of performance testing should be well defined for us". Numbers taken before then moved with things that are not the game: on macOS a covered window, or a screen asleep or locked, draws nothing, and a Retina screen draws at 3200x1800; a debug build adds CPU time; a shell's background jobs ran at lower priority; and scripted runs waited for each chunk of ground every 4 m, a stall play never has. Offscreen, the same scene repeats within a few percent on the same machine, and runs with nobody at it. |
| D259 | Clearing away worktrees | Tech | Agent | Decided | `cargo xtask worktrees` lists every worktree with what it holds; `--prune`, run by the session hook at most once an hour in the background, removes a subagent's worktree (`.claude/worktrees/agent-…`) and its branch once its commits are all on `main`, it has no changes or untracked files, it is not locked and it has been idle two days, and deletes any other subagent's build after two idle days and any other worktree's after three, never its code. Idle is from the last edit to its files, git's record of commits and checkouts, and the build. An agent whose work is pushed runs `cargo xtask worktrees --done` to delete its own build at once (AGENTS.md). Every rule fails toward keeping: anything it cannot read or recognise is left. | Claude Code removes a subagent's worktree when it finishes only if it made no changes, and otherwise after `cleanupPeriodDays`, a month by default, which also deletes transcripts; our agents commit, so theirs stayed, each with a build. A subagent sent another message edits for hours before it commits, so edits count, and a lead resumes its agents many hours later, so two days rather than one (2026-10-14). The agent knows when it is done, the prune is the backstop. Only what git already holds is ever removed, and named session worktrees keep their code, since a session may be resumed there. |
| D319 | Unattended runs take turns on the GPU | Tech | Agent | Decided | Every unattended run of the game (a shot, a film, a benchmark, a sheet, a run driven in the background) waits for an advisory lock on one file, `~/.cache/terra/gpu.lock` (`TERRA_GPU_LOCK` to move it), held until it exits, and prints that it is waiting (`gpu_turn`). A run someone plays never waits. Anything else that renders takes the same turn with `flock ~/.cache/terra/gpu.lock <command>`. A run started under such a turn has it already: on Linux it finds the lock held by one of its forebears (`/proc/locks`) and goes on, as a film script wrapped in `flock` once left its own run waiting on it for half an hour, and every other agent's behind it. | On the developers' machine several agents and sessions render at once while a developer plays on the same GPU: their game stopped responding, a run lost its device, every benchmark was spoiled, and a heavy forest no longer fits twice in the GPU's memory. Taking turns by message between sessions was slow and was forgotten; a lock cannot be. |
| D308 | QA films | Tech | Agent | Decided | `cargo xtask qa <scenario> --set <kit>\|mixes\|all` films a scenario the same every time in a set of kits: a scenario is a file in `tools/sandbox/qa/` (sandbox options, remote calls on given frames, camera moves written against a set); a set is each wall kit in `assets/data/kits/` with its roof, read from the folder, and `mixes`. A scripted run makes the calls on their frames through the remote's own methods (`--calls`), so a film and a script driving a run live do the same thing; it waits for every section, and debris, before each frame, so its frames are the same every run. A first pass live learns how many steps each fill takes in the set, so the film paces them; the film is every second frame at 30 a second with each stage's name; the same calls in a benchmark run write every frame's time (`--frame-times`) and each edit's time to the screen. Building in them costs nothing (`--free-build`: every cost met, claims still holding), and the camera is placed in the base's own metres (`build.look`); a camera a script holds aims no tool. Outputs in `shots/qa/`, an index page of the latest. | The developers, 2026-10-14: "make sure the building systems is fluid, works well, everything adjusts as it gets built (can we preprogram a sequence to capture a video of it that we can do in the future repeatably?)", then "same thing for destruction. i want easily repeatable QA things that will capture videos, and be able to do it per set", and "just the core 'can we construct it with infinite pretend resources'". Calls on frames rather than a script stepping the clock live: a live script's round trips are not frame-locked, and the frames drawn while it waits differ run to run. The castle is put up as the player's base (`build.example`) so an edit to it takes the hammer's path to the screen and is timed there, which no scene did. |
| D249 | No options for one-offs | Tech | Developers | Decided | A command-line option, flag, experiment or tool is added only for work that will come up again. A one-off, such as one scene to picture or one thing to check, is done with what exists: the existing options, driving a run live over the remote protocol (`place`, `look`, `shot`), or a throwaway script that is not committed. When nothing that exists can do it, ask before adding one. Builds on D71: general tools for the work we keep doing, not for each case. | The developers: "generally we don't wanna overly add options for one offs, we should make that into a policy." |
| D254 | Tone mapping for a forest's range | Tech | Agent | Decided | The camera tone maps with Khronos PBR Neutral, saturation at 0.82, in place of AgX (D212): Neutral keeps the saturation AgX drained, and plants' colours were made under AgX, so at full saturation undergrowth read neon. Measured on three forest views at 17:00, AgX held the middle 90% of the picture to 106 of 256 levels whatever else changed (mist, haze or eye adaptation off moved it by two levels at most), so shade under the canopy and the sun flecks in it read alike; Neutral spans 118 and puts the darkest tenth near black (sRGB 15 against 39), the range the forests we look to have. | The developers on 2026-10-13, having played Sons of the Forest: "how the light/shades work, that's sort of the effect we wanna go for"; the second review found the light "flat in value, everything mid-tone". |
| D255 | Gusts | Tech | Agent | Decided | One wind for the grass, the bushes and the trees, as Ghost of Tsushima has it: its strength rises and falls as fronts roll downwind at about 6 m/s, two waves 40 and 90 m long at odd angles, sharpened so calm spells are still and gusts come through as fronts, from a fifth to two and a half times the old steady wind; leaves flutter harder in a gust. The same formula in foliage.wesl, grass.wesl and wind.wesl, so a gust crosses grass and canopy together, and the shadow stand-ins move with it, so the sun flecks shift. | The developers on 2026-10-13, having played Sons of the Forest: "some random effects of light wind ... that's sort of the effect we wanna go for". Measured from a still view over 10 s, the picture's motion between frames ranges 2.1 to 3.7 (the steady wind: 2.4 to 3.5, with no calm). The walk bench holds at 12.0 ms. |
| D256 | Dusk's shade lit by the sky above | Tech | Agent | Decided | The sky light (D190) takes its hue half from the drawn sky and half from the shade's cool colour; while the sun is below about 22°, the shade's share rises to nearly nine tenths at 4°, since the drawn horizon is then orange and pink and the shade under trees is lit by the sky overhead. The warmth stays in the sky and the low sun. | The second review found 18:30 "a flat pink wash". The rest of the warmth is the sun reddened by the air, as at a real dusk, and the bare dirt's own salmon colour, which the floor's work takes on. |
| D326 | Checks for places | Tech | Agent | Decided | Every place a demo makes is checked by: `cargo xtask fields`, a CPU-only run over the Bevy-free crates writing maps and numbers of a region's rules on a date (height, slope, the growing season's temperature, snow, exposure, lakes, each species' fit, the tree line's height) and answering site queries; one table of places (`tools/sandbox/places.ts`) that `vantage`, `lodcheck`, `film`, `pops`, `collide` and the bench scenes take as `--place`; `look.ts` measuring by band (sky, far land, middle, near); and, for settlements, `cargo xtask life`, a game day at a fast clock read over the remote protocol, failing on anyone stuck, through a wall, sliding their feet or late for their schedule, and counting the people, animals and smoke in view. | The developers on 2026-10-15: "whenever we discover something or make a decision that needs to be consistently validated, we document it and build a tool for it". The rules decide every place, so they are checked first, without the GPU; today every view and route is written for the lush forest alone. See [demos.md](demos.md#tools-to-build-shared). |

## Superseded and rejected

Kept so their numbers still resolve. They no longer bind anything.

| # | Topic | Owner | By | Status | Decision | Why, or what closes it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| D5 | Genre | Design | — | Superseded by D187 | Was "sandbox plus simulation, with more depth than Planet Crafter". | D187 says what we are building; Planet Crafter was dropped on 2026-10-12. |
| D6 | Terraforming model | Design | — | Superseded by D89 | A planet divided into cells of local and global values, driving terraforming. | There is no terraforming (D89). The notes, [planet-sim.md](planet-sim.md), and the simulation, `terra_sim`, are parked for reference. |
| D16 | Order of hostile life | Design | — | Superseded by D103 | Plants, then insect swarms, then animals. | Monsters form from pooled magic, beside ordinary wildlife (D103). |
| D19 | Three.js as primary stack | Tech | — | Rejected | Three.js in the browser as the primary stack. | It is a renderer, not an engine; the CPU-heavy core would need WASM anyway; the browser is a poor co-op host; no precedent at this scope. |
| D22 | Languages | Tech | — | Superseded by D121 | Rust for everything, with no scripting language. | D121 keeps Rust for the game and adds TypeScript for tooling outside it. |
| D24 | Water in the first version | Design | — | Superseded by D75 | One global sea level that rises as ice melts; no flowing water. | The sea is D75, and with no terraforming (D89) nothing melts to raise it. |
| D27 | Build in Rust instead of Godot | Design | — | Superseded by D11, D12 | Rebuild the game in Rust rather than carry on in Godot. | Decided on 2026-10-06; now part of D11 (everything in Rust) and D12 (Bevy, with the Godot build retired). |
| D39 | What the engine must do regardless of gameplay | Design | — | Superseded by D8, D9, D40, D187 | Was: voxel worlds, resources harvested organically, procedural generation almost everywhere. | Each part is now its own Decided item: procedural content (D8), smooth diggable voxels (D9), materials in the ground (D40), and the engine as a whole (D187). |
| D44 | Melting into pools of water | Design | — | Superseded by D75, D89 | Ice melting into local pools of water. | Nothing melts without terraforming (D89), and the sea is D75. Water that differs by place stays possible, since the engine asks for water by position (D45). |
| D50 | Style of play | Design | — | Superseded by D89, D97, D180 | Broadly like No Man's Sky or Subnautica: a player with tools, gathering resources and building. | The game is now a living world with RPG in it (D89), in third person (D97), with Enshrouded's combat as its baseline (D180). |
| D55 | Carrying and cost | Design | — | Superseded by D139, D141, D106 | Was: the player carries raw items, and those let them build what they can. | Building is a plan built with materials brought to it (D139), drawn from storage nearby as well as what you carry (D141); what you carry is in your pack (D106). |
| D56 | How construction is put together | Design | — | Superseded by D152, D154, D181 | Buildables as list entries with named placement rules and looks that follow their neighbours; rooms as plain boxes. | Rooms are gone: parts are walls, floors and roofs (D152), placed the Sims' way (D154), as cubes on the grid (D181). Kinds as data stay (D136). |
| D57 | The building grid | Design | — | Superseded by D152, D154, D181 | One half-metre grid for everything, with fixed-size buildables such as a round room joined by connectors. | The half-metre grid stays, as D181's cubes; round rooms and connectors went with rooms (D152, D154). |
| D60 | Terraforming | Design | — | Superseded by D89 | Probably no terraforming, with the notes kept. | D89 settled it: no terraforming. [planet-sim.md](planet-sim.md) and `terra_sim` are parked. |
| D65 | Inventory and backpack | Design | — | Superseded by D106 | Was: the inventory is the backpack, small at the start, made bigger by a bigger backpack. | Settled by the developers on 2026-10-12 as D106: a pack is a container, and a bigger one holds more. |
| D79 | Terraform rating | Design | — | Superseded by D89 | A terraformable planet's rating of how far along it is. | There is no terraforming (D89). |
| D209 | How roofs are laid and drawn | Tech | — | Superseded by D241 | Was: a kit laid pitched roofs from roof sections, rectangles of a style and pitch over a storey's walls, the highest at each point, each cell going up a stage at a time; a roof the shortcuts put up in the kind of the walls under it. | D226 asked for roofs built by hand; roofs are roof cubes (D241), which keep its lattice, creases, courses and pieces. |

D177 restated with its two exceptions (D197, D200). `terra_build`'s layouts split into `kit`, `layout` (`runs`, `trims`, `ground`, `floors`, `blocks`), `timber`, `draw` and `relief` (D195, step 2). Kit materials reflect 2% rather than 4%, so the sky no longer sheens them blue in shade.
